The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
894.11.4 narcolies/8
Instruction No. 70, of June 16, 1930, quoting an
article in the WASHINGTON HERALD of April 29, 1930,
and an article in the LADIES HOME JOURNAL for June.
These articles referred to an alleged publication
known as the SPOT GOODS REPORTER of Yokohama.
So far as the Embassy has been able to ascertain,
the SPOT GOODS REPORTER ceased publication in 1922 or
1923.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
1923. Since that time there has appeared occasionally,
I understand, a mimeographed or roughly printed sheet
devoted principally to advertising the sale of narcotic
drugs in bulk. This publication is, I believe, sur-
reptitious; at least, the Japanese authorities are said to
profess ignorance of its existence. As the Department
is aware, the open sale of large quantities of narcotic
drugs would not be sanctioned by the Japanese authorities.
I have no reason to believe that great amounts of
narootios are manufactured in Japan for the illicit trade.
It is possible that there is some leakage from Japanese
factories, but I am convinced that it is not sanotioned
by the authorities and that the Japanese Government is
making a sincere effort to control manufacture and dis-
tribution. In times past Japanese ports have been used
as depots for the storage of narcotic drugs intended for
reexport. This, I believe, has been particularly true
of Kobe, which carries on a large transshipment business.
Up until a few years ago the Japanese made no effort to
investigate or control goods deposited in bond for reexport.
They have correoted this to a certain extent, it is stated,
by requiring export and import permits from the country
of origin or destination, respectively, on all narcotics
deposited in bond. It is possible that the narcotics are
falsely manifested and deposited in bond in the trans-
shipment warehouses. Transshipment of these falsely
manifested goods can then be made by local agents.
This traffio is illicit and the authorities have the
same diffioulty with it that attaches to it elsewhere.
Iam

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
I am sometimes inclined to think that the local
administration is not as effective as it might be, but
I do not believe that it is due to any desire on the
part of the Government to wink at practices which the
country is under treaty obligation, supplemented by domestic
legislation, to suppress.
I have the honor to be, Sir,
Your obedient servant,
Eluin t. twith
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

Sir:
1/.
I have the honor to enclose copies in translation
supplied by the Foreign Office, of Department of Home
Affairs Ordinance No. 17, of May 19, 1930, entitled
Regulations for the Control of Narootic Drugs. These
Regulations are designed to give effeot to the Narcotics
Convention conoluded at Geneva in 1925.
These Regulations are a decided advanoe on the
legislation hitherto obtaining . A record of trans-
actions is required, and a striot control over imports
and exports is provided for, as well as a system of
inspection

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
inspeotion. The Regulations also include Indian Hemp
(hashish) among the dangerous drugs. The Regulations,
however, do not call for an accounting of sales of less
than five grams. This provision, or omission, appears to
leave open sufficient supply for individual addicts. The
Japanese authorities believe, however, that there are no
Japanese addicts.
I have been informed that these Regulations go as
far as publio opinion permits. The fact is, I think, that
Japanese administration lags considerably behind the enter-
prise of Japanese manufacturers and dealers.
Respectfully yours,
Eduim P. hivilly,
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.
Enclosure:
Copy in translation of
Ordinance No. 17 relating to
Control of Narcotic Drugs.

Department for Home Affairs Ordinance No. 17
issued on May 19, 1930.
Regulations for the control of Narcotic
Drugs.
Art. I. By the term narcotic drugs in the present
Ordinance are understood any of the substances specified in
the following items:
1. Morphine, diacetyl-morphine and cocaine ( regardless
of the degree of specific rotation ) and salts
thereof.
2.
Crude morphine, coca leaves and crude cocaine.
3. Ecgonine ( regardless of the degree of specific
rotation) and derivatives and salts thereof.
4.
Morphine derivatives and salts ( except codeine.
dihydro-codeine and salts thereof).
5. Substances, in which more than two-thcusandths of
morhpine or morphine derivatives(except codeine and
dihydro-codeine), more than one-thousandth of cocaine,
eogonine or cogonine derivatives or diacetyl-morphine,
is deteoted.
6. Indian hemp, resin thereof and substances containing it.
7.

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
(2)
7. Substanoes designated by the Minister of Home Affairs.
Art. II. A person proposing to manufacture narcotic drugs
(except coca leaves) specified in items No. 1,2 and 3 of the
preceding article or narcotic drugs designated by the Minister
of Home Affairs as those for manufacturing which permission
shall be obtained in accordance with the provisions of the
present Ordinance, shall apply for permission to the inister
of Home Affairs through the local Governor of the place in
which he has his factory( in Tokyo Prefecture, in this and
the following cases, the Inspector-General of the Metro-
politan Police) by presenting a statement giving the matters
specified in the following items, nd in regard to the
matter specified in item No. 3, attaching thereto a plan
of it:
1. Locality of the factory.
2. Names of the drugs.
3. Position, structure and equipment of the building to
be used as factory or storehouse.
When it is desired to change any of the matters specified
in the above items after the permission mentioned in the
preceding clause has been obtained, further permission shall
be obtained for the said matter.
Art. III. A person who has obtained the permission
mentioned in Clause I of the preceding article shall annually
obtain permission of the Minister of Home Affairs through the
local Governor with regard to the quantity he proposes to
manufaoture, the description and quantity of the material
he will use and the method of obtaining it.
Art. IV. A person proposing to manufacture any of the
narootio drugs specified in items from No. 4 to No. 7 of
Art. 1, exoept those designated hy the Minister of Home
Affairs

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
(3)
Affairs as those for manufacturing which permission shall
be obtained, shall present to the Linister of Home Affairs
through the local governor of the district in which he has
his factory a report giving the matters specified in the
following items:-
1. Locality of the factory.
2.
Name of the drug.
3.
Estimated amount of the substance to be
manufactured during the year.
Description and quantity of the material to be
used and the method of obtaining it.
When it is desired to change any of the matters specified
on the above items after the report mentioned in the preceding
clause has been presented, a further report shall be presented
on the said matter.
Art. V. A person who has manufactured narcotic drugs
shall annually present a report to the Minister of Home Affairs
through the looal governor of the district, in which he has his
factory, giving the names and quantities of the drugs he has
manufactured before the end of December of that year, the
descriptions, quantities and places of origin of the materials
he has used, the report to be presented before the end of
January of the following year.
The provision of the preceding clause is applied to the
manufacturer of non-narcotio substances, which have been
manufactured using narcotic drugs as material.
Art. VI. A person desiring to cultivate coca shrubs for
the purpose of colleoting coca leaves shall obtain permission
of the Minister of Home Affairs through the local governor of
the district in which he has his plantation, by presenting a
statement giving the matters specified in the
following

the following items:-

3. Estimated quantity of coca leaves to be annually

When it is desired to change any of the matter specified

A person who has obtained the permission mentioned in

of the following year.

Art. X. A person proposing to export to foreign
countries or Japanese colonies narcotic drugs shall obtain
permi ssion from the Minister of Home Affairs through the local
governor of the district in which he has head business office
by presenting a statement giving the matters specified in each
of the following items, attaching thereto the import certi-
ficate from foreign countries or Japanese colonies or the
certificate approving the introduction of the consignment
into the honded warehouse, which has been issued by the
authorities of the country to which they ure destined:-
1. Names and quantities of the drugs.
2. Personal name( in the case of a juridical person, its
name) or trade-name of the consignee and the locality
of his business office.
3. Term of exportation to foreign countries or Japanese
colonies.
4. Method of transportation.
5. Port of export to foreign countries or Japanese
colonies(in case goods are sent by post, the name
of the post office.)
When it is desired to change any of the items specified in
the preceding clause after the permission mentioned in the
preceding clause has been obtained, further permission shall
be obtained for the said matter.
On the permission mentioned in clause I being granted, an
export authorization to foreign countries or Japanese colonies
and a copy of the first mentioned shall be handed over.
The copy of the export authorization to foreign countries
mentioned in the preceding clause shall accompany the consign-
men.

Art. XI. The master of a vessel carrying on board

. head of the Customs House of the port on the occasion of the

diversion certificate which accompanies the consignment.
The provision of the preceding clause is not a plied to
transportation made through post.

narcotic drugs, which are foreign goods, shall present to the
head of the Customs House concerned a copy of the export
authorization or the diversion certificate which accchpenies
the consignment.

Art. XVI. A person selling narcotic drugs specified in
Items No. 1, No. 3 or No. 4 in Art.I or selling them in small
portions shall statre on their vessels or envelopes his personal

not applied to a vessel or an envelope containing not
exceeding 5 grams.
Art.XVII. When a dealer in drugs transfers arcotic
drugs to another dealer in drugs, a physician, a dentist
or a veterinary surgeon, he shall obtain from the other
party a document certified by the Chief of the Police Station
that he is a dealer in drugs, a physician, a dentist or a
veterinary surgeon. When, however, he transfers narootio
drugs to another dealer in drugs, a physician, a dentist
or a veterinary surgeon, with whom he is acquainted, this
rule is not applied.

Despatoh No. 122
The Oseka Nalmtchl
From American Embassy
Osake, Wednesdmy, Janaary 21, 1981.
Tokyo.
Coded Cables Seized
Reveal Big Dope Ring
Extensive Dope Traffic With
London as Headquarters
Claimed by Police
YOKOHAMA, Jan 20.—More than
100 coded cablegrams were seized
yesterday by Procurator Ito of the
Yokohama District Court from a
wholesale druggist of Edobashi,
Osaka, enabling the authorities to
bare the illicit drug traffic by inter-
national "dope peddlers" and their
methods of sinuggling narcotics by
air routes.
The drug traffickers with their
headquarters in London, by offer-
ing fabulous prices to manufac-
turing druggists of France, Bel-
gium, and Turkey, would smug-
gle large quantities of morphine,
opium, cocaine and heroin aboard air-
ships' or 'airpfanes operating oh re-
gular airways.
After carrying drugs beyond the
boundaries of the respective countrics
of origin, they would load them on
steamers plying between London and
Yokohama. At the latter port, the
smugglers' agents through some frau-
dulent method, would deceive customs
inspectors into certifyiig entries.
Since 1927
The drugs that passed the customs
inspection at Yokohama were all car-
ried via air route to Dairen, Shang-
hai, and Hongkong. Even conserva-
tively estimated, the drugs smuggled
in this fashiop since 1927, exceeded
in. value ¥80,00,000. What was
smuggled prior to that is hard to
estimate, it is declared.
For passing the customs inspectors
at Yokohama, the smugglers devised
a chemical camouflage by which. the
narcotics were said to have function-
ed as though they were something
else:
Besides, the "camouflaged narco-
tics" were packed side by side with
chemivals,, making it doubly diffcult
to tcll them from the legitimate ar-
ticles. One consignment is said to
have weighed from: 1,000 -to 2,500
pounds.
Traced by Scotland Yard
It was the bankruptcy of two large
druggists in London, each capitaliz-
ed at more than £1,000,000 and each
backed by. influential British politi-
cians, which: enabled Scotland Yard
to trace the illicit traffic by the in-
ternational drug ring and to round
up four leaders last fall. Action here
was taken at the request of Scotland
Yard made: through the British Em-
bacsy.
The illicit. traffic. on the Far East-
ern end was said to have been look-
ed after by a German, who when he
sensed that the authorities were after
him, disappeared.
This discovery is xpected, to en-
liven the forthcoming international
narcotic conference at Geneva. Be-
catuse air routes have been resorted
to in the illicit traffic, customs in-
spection nt airports from- now on is
expected to becom- much stricter.

LAWS AND REGULATIONS REGARDING THE
CONTROL OF THE TRAFFIC IN NARCOTIC DRUGS.
To
American Diplomatic Officers
and Certain American Consular Officers.
Sirs:
Supplementing tho Department's telegraphic circular
instruction of August 28, 1929, to diplomatic officers,
there are enolosed herewith, in duplicate, questionnaires
in which have been listed the narcotics laws and regula-
tions received by the Department as now in force in the
894.114 NARCOTICS/6
country to which you are accredited, a summary of their
provisions, and questions indicating the nature of the
additional information which the Department desires to
obtain. A blank set of the questionnaire forms is also
enclosed.
Similar questionnaires are being sent, with this
circular instruction, to the consular officers in colonies,
possessions and mandated territories shown in the annexed
list. Questionnaire forms for possessions, colonies,
dependencies and mandated territories in which no consular
officers are stationed are being sent to the appropriate
diplomatic missions.
The information referred to is required in official
form for the use of the Commissioner of Narcotics to
guide his aotion in the enforcement of the laws of the
United States governing the export and import of narcotic
drugs, under treaties to which this Government is a party.
It wiil also be valuable to the United States Government
in connection with international aspects of control of
the traffic in narcotic drugs. It is expected that,
eventually, all of a non-confidential nature will be
compiled in one printed document suitable for reference
purposes, and that copies of such document will be trans-
mitted to the Governments signatory to The Hague Convention
and to any other interested Governments.
In order that the data may be exact and complete,
and may include everything available to assist in a
complete understanding of the method of governmental
control of the narcotio drug traffic in all of the terri-
tories,

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
tories, including possessions, dependencies, protectorates
and mandated areas assigned to you for report by the
present instruction, you are instructed to bring the
onclosed questionnaires to the attontion of the Govern
ment to which you are accredited, with the request that
the competent authority be so good as to rectify any
orrors in the entries thereon and to add any pertinent
information.
In the questionnaires covering dominions, colonics,
possossions, dependencies and mandated territories, the
laws and regulations have not been summarized, owing to
the incompleteness of the Department's information. No
effort has been made to list all such areas, but, in each
case where the Department's files contain laws and regula-
tions understood to be in forcc in any of thosc areas,
lists of such laws and regulations have becn embodicd in
the appropriate questionnaires.
Diplomatic missions are instructed to make an effort
to obtain from the Governments to which they are accredited
complete lists of the laws and regulations of all of their
dominions, colonies, possessions, dependencies and mandated
territories, excepting those assigned to other officers
under this instruction, together with copies of laws and
regulations which the Department has not received. It will
be appreciated if the Government will also undertake to
summarize such laws and regulations on the questionnaires
separately for each area.
Diplomatic missions should also transmit a list of
all dominions, colonies, possessions, dependencies, por-
teotorates and mandated territories of the Governments
to which they are accredited, concerning whose laws and
regulations they have been unable to obtain the desired
information.
It should be borne in mind that, in addition to the
information contained in or submitted in response to the
questionnaires, the Department desires to receive for its
files four copies (with English text or translation if
possible) of any laws or regulations on the subject which
are now in effect but have not hitherto been transmitted,
or which may be issued hereafter.
Very truly yours,

Laws and Regulations.
1. What laws, regulations, etc., are in force?
(a) Regulations for the Sale and Treatment of
Medisines, Law No. 10 or l889, as revised by
Laws No. 6 of 1892, No. 6 of 1899, No. 35 of
1907 and No. 24 of 1910.
(b) The Opium Law, Law No. 27 of 1897, as rovised
by Laws No. 26 of 1917 and No. 43 of 1919.
Law, Ordinance No. 4, of l897, as revised by
Ordinance No. 4 or 1919.
(a)
Order No. 6, dated August 14, 1917, of the
Department of Home Affairs, relative to the
Government sale of opium to be used for
medicinal purposes.
(continued on page la)
B.
Substances Controlled.
1. What narcotic drugs are controlled by these
laws and regulations?
All the drugs specified in the Hague and Geneva
Conventions, and all derivatives and preparations
of these drugs which may have the same harmful
effect and are designated by the Minister for
Home Affairs.

which are not included in the Conventionst

The Government has a complete monopoly of the trade,
both internal and external, in raw and medicinal
opium. Connected with the monopoly is the Imperial
Hygienie Laboratory of Tokyo with a branch at
Osaka, and into these laboratories for storage or
for manufacture of medicinal opium goes all the
opium imported and all that is purchased from the
poppy farmers of Japan. These farmers are licensed
by the Prefectural Governors and must deliver to
them all the opium produoed. The governors in
turn ferward the opium to the laboratories. The
monopoly is financed by means of an ordinary bud-
get of the Gcternment, but the expenses of the ad-
ministration of the law regulating the commeree
in the derivatives of opium, and in coca leaves
and coeaine, are inoluded in the appropriation

monopely and so a limitation 1s effected on the
amount of morphine that may be manufactured in Japan.
The medicinal opium manufactured by the monopoly is sent through
the Provineial Governers te specially appointed and qualified

From American Embaasy
To:yo.
Questionnaire.
Legal and Administrative Measures for the Control of
the Traffic in Narcotic Drugs ( Japan Proper) .
I. General
A.
Laws and Regulations.
1. What laws, regulations, etc., are in force?
(a) Regulations for the Sale and Treatment of
Medicines, Laws No. 10 of 1889, as revised by
Laws No. 6 of 1892, No. 6 of 1899, No. 35 of
1907, No. 24 of 1910 and No. 44 of 1925.
(b)
The Opium Law, Law No. 27 of 1897, as revised by
Laws No. 26 of 1917 and No. 43 of 1919.
(c)
Regulations for the Enforcement of the Opium Law,
Home Office Ordinance No. 4 of 1897, as revised
by Ordinances No. 4 of 1919, No. 5 of 1922 and
No. 44 of 1928.
(a)
Home Office Ordinance No. 6 of 19l7, relative
to the Government Sale of Opium to be Used for
Manufacturing Alkaloids.
(e)
Regulations for the Control of Transportation of
Opium by Ships, Department of Comnunications
Ordinance No. 5 of 1930.
(f)
Regulations for the Control of Narcotic Drugs, Home
Office Ordinance No. 17 of 1930.
The Government has a complete monopoly of the trate,
both internal and external, in raw and medicinal opium.
Connected with the monopoly are the Imperial Hygienio

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Laboratory of Tokio and that of Osaka, and into these
laboratories for storage or for manufacture of medicinal
opium goes all the opium imported and all that is purchased
by the Government from the poppy farmers of Japan. These
farmers are to be licensed by the Prefectural Governor, and
also must obtain, every year by the date fixed by the
Governor, his permission with regard to the locality and
acreage of poppy cultivation. The farmers must deliver
all the opium produced to the Governor, who in turn
forwards the opium to the laboratories.
The monopoly is
financed by means of an ordinary budget of the Government,
but the expenses of the administration of the laws
regulating the commerce in the derivatives of opium, and
in coca leaves and cocaine, are included in the appropria-
tion for the control of dangerous and other medicines.
The Minister for Home Affairs, in whose department is
lodged control of the monopoly and of the commercial trade
in opium and coca leaves and their derivatives, has
authorized only four concerns to purchase opium from the
Government monopoly. As no person or firm may procure
raw opium from any other source, only the firms specially
appointed can get raw material from which to manufacture
morphine.
The medicinal opium manufactured by the monopoly is
sent through the Prefectural Governor to the agents
specially appointed and qualified who supply, in their
Prefecture, qualified physicians, dentists, veterinary
surgeons, pharmacists and manufacturers of medicine. If
the purchaser, even though licensed to handle habit-
forming

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
L
-3-
forming drugs, wishes to purchase more than fifty
grams, he must obtain a certificate from his Pre-
fectural Governor; if less than fifty grams, from the
local police authorities. In preparing medicine, a
qualified person can obtain without the above men-
tioned certificate five receptacles of five grams
each within one year. Medicinal opium may not be trans-
ferred from one prefecture to another.
B. Substances Controlled.
1. What narcotic drugs are controlled by these laws and
regulations?
(1). All the drugs specified in the Hague and Geneva Conventions,
(and other drugs to which the Geneva Convention may be
applied in accordance with its Article l0).
(2) other drugs.
(3) Substances designated by the Minister for Home Affairs.
Note. What are the drugs covered by the laws which are not
included in the Convention?
(1) All the drugs decided as narcotic drugs in accordance with
Artiole 10 of the Convention.
(2) Other derivatives of morphine and of eogonine.
C. Administration.
1. What departments of the Government are charged with the
administration of the marcotic laws?
There are approximately fifty prefectures in Japan Proper.
At the head of each is a Governor appointed by the National
Government and his position is like that of civil servant
in Great Britain. He takes orders from the Central
Government

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Government and is responsible to it. Appointed also by
the Central Government is the head of the Prefeotural Police
Bureau.
The control of the monopoly and of the trade in opium, coca
leaves and their derivatives, is lodged in the Department of
Home Affairs, familiarly called " The Central Authority".
In order to facilitate cooperation, an Opium Commission has
been created consisting of the Minister for Home Affairs, the
Vice-Ministers and Directors of the Ministries concerned, and
the Vice-Governors of the Colonies.
There are specially appointed "inspectors" in tne prefectures.
They enlist the services of the local police to assist them
in the actual work of inspection. Since there are ll4,259
persons or firms authorized to handle or to prescribe drugs,
it is impossible for the Governor Inspectors to do this work
unaided. These instrumentalities, together with the harbor
or "Water police", who are an official part of the customs
administration, make up Japan's machinery for control. At
the same time the railway administration, postal service,
also cooperate with those authorities of the principal
departments.
2. If administration is not centralized in one department, what
provision is made for coordination?
D. Right to Deal in Narcotics.
1. Manufacture, Wholesale and Retail .
In this country a distinction practically exists between
wholesale and retail dealers in narcotics, but in regard
to the matter of control they are not governed by any
disoriminatory laws and regulations.
Persons

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Persons who may manufacture and sell narcotics are
confined as a rule to pharmacists(who possess fixed
qualifications and having had licenses granted by the
Minister for Home Affairs, have their names registered
in the official list of pharmacists). With regard to
the manufacture of narcotics, persons undertaking it are
required to obtain special permission from the Minister
for Home Affairs in accordance with the Regulations
for the Control of Narcotic Drugs.
There are "manufactures of drugs" licensed by the
prefectural governor, but theyaabsolutely prohibited
from selling any drugs other than those manufactured by
them . (As for the manufacture of narcotics such persons
are required, as pharmacists, to obtain permission from
the Minister for Home Affairs.) There are also "aruggists",
who may sell drugs, but can not be permitted to manufacture
them. As for the sale and transference of narcotics, it
is permitted only between them.
2. Medicinal Opium.
The manufacture and whole sale of medicinal opium can
be exclusively undertaken by the Government, and sale of it
is done only by persons specially designated as "dealers in
medicinal opium" by the Government among pharmacists and
druggists.

4. Importers and Exporters. - Is the right to import and export
narcotic drugs restricted to any comme cial or professional
groups?
The import or export of narcotics may be done by
persons who have obtained permission from the Minister
for Home Affairs each time it is done. Accordingly the
total number of importers or exporters is not fixed.
5. Brokers. - Is there any legislation restricting the right
of any person within the country to negotiate purchases and
sales between persons or firms in foreign countries?
None.
E. Licenses.
1.
Are special narcotic licenses required for dealing in
narootio drugs?
The manufacture of narcotics may be done by pharmacists

=== Page 78 === (confidence 1.00)
-7-
and manufacturers of drugs and (i) for manufacturing
those mentioned under (b), (c) and (g) of Article IV
of the Geneva Convention as well as those designated
by the Minister for Home Affairs, they are required to
obtain licenses from the Minister for Home Affairs and
(2) for manufacturing all other narcotics, they are
required to report before starting their manufacture.
2.
What department of the Government is authorized to issue
and revoke licenses?
Granting of licenses concerning the manufacture,
import, export etc. of narcotics and cancellation of
such licenses are effected by the Minister for Home Affairs.
3.
What requirements must be satisfied by the applicant before
licenses may be issued?
(i) A person who manufactures narcotics shall apply for
licenses to the Minister for Home Affairs throuh the
prefectural governor by presenting a statement giving
the particulars specified in the following items:-
1. Locality of the factory.
2.
Names of the drugs.
3.
Positions, structures and equipment of the
building to be used as factory or storehouse.
(ii) A person who imports narcotics shall obtain a license
from the Minister for Home Affairs through the pre-
fectural Governor by presenting a statement giving
the particulars specified in each of the following
items:-
1. Names and quantities of the drugs.
2.
Object of importation.
3.
Name and address of the consignor.
4. Period for importation.
5.

=== Page 79 === (confidence 1.00)
-8-
5. Means of transportation.
6. Port of importation.
(iii) A person who exports narcotics shall obtain a license
from the Minister for Home Affairs through the pre-
fectural Governor by presenting a statement giving the
particulars specified in each of the following items:
1. Names and quantities of the drugs.
2. Name and address of the consignee.
3. Period for exportation.
4. Means of transportation.
5. Port of export.
4. What investigation is made concerning such matters as
reliability, needs and premises of the applicants?
The Minister for Home Affairs issues orders to the
prefectural governor to carry out the investigation
referred to.
5. Under what circumstances may the administrative authority
revoke licenses?
In the event of a person infringing the provisions
of the Regulations for the Control of Narcotic Drugs or
being guilty of an offence concerning narcotic drugs or
opium or having committed any illegal acts his license
may be cancelled or he may be prohibited from manufacturing.
Section
Section
(Cf. 16 of Pharmacist Law, 42 of Regulations for the Sale
and Treatment of Medicines)
6. What method is employed to keep manufacturers and whole-
sale's informed of the names of the licensed dealers to whom
sales may be made?
When a dealer in drugs transfers narcotics to another
dealer in drugs or a physician he shall demand a certificate
issued by the Chief of the Police Station certifying that the
buyer

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
buyer is a dealer in drugs or a physician, except when a
dealer in drugs transfers narcotics to another dealer in
drugs or a physician, who is personally known t. the dealer
in drugs. ( This certificate shall be preserved for five
Years).
F.
Post.
1. Is the country a member of the Universal Postal Union?
Yes.
2. Under what circumstances may drugs be sent through the
post?
Forwarding of narcotics by post may be done by packing
them in a box with value declared and by parcel post, be-
sides the import or export authorization required to be
presented.
G.
Taxation.
1. Is there any system of taxation of the importation and ex-
portation(exclusive of customs duties), the manufacture
or sale of narcotic drugs?
None.

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
II
Import and Export.
A. Certificate System.
1. What import and export certificate system has been adopted?
(i.e. Is the system prescribed by Chapter 5 of the
Geneva Convention used in full; if not, what are the
principal features of the system in use?)
Japan has put in force the import and export
certificate system which has been adopted by a large
number of countries on the reconmendation of the League
of Nations Opium Advisory Committee, and no consignment
of any of the substances covered by the Hague Convention
or any of their derivatives may be imported or exported
without a certificate. Raw opium and medicinal opium may
be imported or exported only by the Government monopoly.
The policy of the monopoly is to restrict the export
of medicinal opium to Japanese doctors, dentists and
pharmacists in foreign countries who can produce certified
import authorization certificates issued by the Japanese
Consul of the district in which the importer lives.
The
law does not forbid the importation of cocaine and
morphine but it is the resent policy of the Goverrment
to refuse all applications for import certificates for
these drugs so that the internal factories are the only
legitimate sources of supply.
2.
What authority is charged with the duty of issuing import
and export certificates?
The Minister for Home Affairs.
B. Imports.
1. Under what circumstances will an import certificate be
issued?
An

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
An import certificate is issued when a pharmacist or
a druggist has obtained authorization from the Minister
of Home Affairs for importing narcotics by applying therefor
through the Governor of the locality whereat he keeps
his main office.
2.
How many copies of the import certificate are issued and
what disposition is made of them:
When the Minister of Home Affairs issues an import
certificate to an importer of narcotics, he takes steps
to have a copy of the certificate sent through the Foreign
Office to the Japanese diplomatic authorities stationed
in the country from which the import is made, so that it
may be referred to by the Governaent of that country.
Furthermore, every time authorization for import of
narcotics is granted, notice to that effect is sent to
the Customs authorities at the port Whereat the narcotics
are landed.
This double check will go very far in preventing
any fraudulent transactions on the certificate issued by
the Japanese authority.
3.
What limits, if any, are imposed on the amounts that may be
imported?
There are no limits set on the amounts of narcotics that
may be imported by licensed persons, but in actual practice,
amounts are limited as much as possible, and since 1929
the policy has been taken to make an annual diminution of
the total amount of coca leaves and of crude cocaine im-
ported in order to reduce the output of the cocaine to the
level of the country's consumption plus the amount of
legitimate exports.
4.
Through what oustoms offices may imports be effected?
There is no regulation relating to the restriction on the
number of customs offices through which imports may be
efccted

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
effected, but practically all are cleared through Yokohama,
Kobe, Osaka, Moji and Hakodate.
5.
What officials or departments of the Government, local or
national, are notified that an import certificate has
been issued, and by what means is such notification efrected?
The Foreign Office is notified by means of the transmission
to them of a copy of the import certificate, while the
customs are notified of the authorization of importation
by the Home Office.
6. How long are import certificates valid?
Generally they are valid for six months.
7.
In case export certificates accompany imported con-
signments, are such certificates returned to the countries
of origin and, if so, by whom and with what endorsement?
The customs do not retur the export certificate to the
issuing country. The copy of the export authorization
sent by the exporting Govermnent is returned to that
Government with the endorsement in accordance with the
article 13 of the Geneva Convention.
8.
Do the customs keep separate records of the imports of
narcotic drugs?
Yes.
Do the customs notify the authority which issued the import
certificate of the arrival of the consignment and the exact
amount entered, and, if so, how soon after entry?
When import is made the importer is required to report
the fact within ten days to the Minister of Home Affairs
through the prefectural Governor by appending to the
report the import authorization and a copy of the export
certificate or of the diversion certificate attached to the
imported

=== Page 84 === (confidence 1.00)
-13-
imported goods. The customs authorities endorse the
import authorization referred to after comparing it with
the notice of authorization for import received from the
Minister of Home Affairs beforehand. In this way, though
the Customs authorities do not notify the Minister
airectly of the fact of import, the latter may see whether
the import has been made or not.
10.
Are records of imports kept by the authority which issued
the certificates?
Yes.
11. Are the internal customs or any other authorities at the
place where the consignment is to be cleared and released
notified of its arrival at the external customshouse?
See 9.
12. May narcotics be imported under such general descriptions
as " Pharmaceutical Products", and, if not, what description
is required?
Narcotics can not be imported under such general descriptions.
In all cases of import, accompanying papers must show the
exact nature of the shipment.
13.
May narcotics be imported on bills-of-lading made out
"to order"?
Regardless of bills-of-lading, Customs deliver narcotics
imported to the person only who obtained the importation
authorization.
14.
May narcotics be imported in cases containing other
pharmaceutical products?
Yes.
C.

=== Page 85 === (confidence 1.00)
-14-
C.
Exports.
1. Under what circumstances are export certificates issued?
In export trade, dealers in drugs can obtain authoriza-
tion of exportation from the Minister for Home Affairs
through the prefectural Governor by presenting a state-
ment giving the particulars specified in the following
items, attaching thereto the import certificate issued
by the authorities of the country of destination or the
certificate approving the introduction of the consign-
ment into the bonded warehouse;
1. Names and quantities of the drugs.
2.
Personal name( in the case of a judicial person, its
name) or trade-name of the consignee and the
address of his business office.
3.
Period for exportation to foreign countries or to
the territory.
4.
Means of transportation.
5.
Port of export to foreign countries or to the
territory.
2.
What policy does the issuing authority pursue regarding
applications for permission to export to:
(a) Countries which are parties to a narcotic drugs
convention but which have not adopted the import
certificate system ?
(0)
Countries which are not parties to a narcotic
drugs convention and whioh have not adopted the
import and export certificate system?
In both cases the Minister for Home Affairs issues
the export authorization on condition that an
applicant

=== Page 86 === (confidence 1.00)
-15-
applicant presents the import certificate issued by the
authority of the country of destination or the certificate
approving the introduction of the consignment into the bonded
warehouse.
3.
Through what customs offices may exports be effected?
Exports may be effected through any customs office, but
practically all consignments are exported through the
customs houses at Yokohama, Kobe, Osaka, Moji, and Hakodate.
4.
What officials or departments of the Government, local or
national, are notified that an export certificate has been
issued, and by what means is such notification effected?
The Custom Office concerned is notified by the Home Office in
an official document that an export certificate has been
issued.
5.
How many copies of the export certificate are issued and what
disposition is made of them?
The Minister for Home Affairs grants to the applicant the export
authorization, and a copy of the export authorization which the
applicant attaches to the consignment. He prepares another
copy of the export authorization which is sent for endorsement,
through the Foreign Office, to the Government of destination
in accordance with the provision of the Geneva Convention.
6.
How long are export certificates valid?
Generally for six months.
7.
Do the customs keep separate records of the exports of
narcotio drugs?
Yes.
8.

=== Page 87 === (confidence 1.00)
-16-
8. Do the customs notify the authority which issued the
export certificate, or any other authority, when ex-
ported consignments are cleared?
A person who has exported narcotics, reports it within
10 days to the Minister for Home Affairs through the pre-
fectural governor, attaching to the report the exporta-
tion authorization with the necessary endorsement by the
customs office. This method assumes that the Home Office
is notified indirectly by the customs office of the
execution of the exportation.
Are records of exports keptythe authority which issues
9.
the certificates?
Yes.
10.
May narcotics be exported under such general descriptions
as
" Pharmaceutical Products", and, if not, what
description is required?
In all cases of export, accompanying papers must show
the exact nature of the shipment. The exporter must
attach to the consignment a copy of the exportation
authorization.
11.
May narcotics be exported on bills-of-lading made out
" to order"?
Regardless of bills-of-lading, Customs do not allow
narcotics to be exported except by the person who obtained
the exportation authorization.
12.
May narcotics be exported in cases containing other
pharmaceutical products?
Yes.
13.

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
13. What investigation is made if the importing country
does not return the copy of the export certificate
within a reasonable time?
The question is to be examined by the Permanent Central
Board at Geneva.
14.
Are imports and exports reported either to the Netherland
Government or to the League of Nations; and, if so, do the
reports specify the actual dates of departure from or
arrival in the country?
The Japanese Government reports imports and exports
four times in a year to the Permanent Central Board
at Geneva, but does not specify the actual dates of
departure or arrival.

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
III. Transit and Diversion.
A. Transit.
1. Under what circumstances may consignments pass in
transit through the country?
(i) The master of a vessel carrying on board narcotic
drugs sent from one foreign country to another is
not permitted to transport the narcotic drugs for
the purpose of passing through Japan, unless he
presents to the head of the Customs of the port
at the time of the vessel's entrance therein
a copy of the export authorization or the diversion
certificate which accompanies the consignment.
(ii) A person can land(for storage in a honded ware-
house) ,reship or transship, narcotics of foreign
consignment, by presenting to the head of the
Customs concerned a copy of the export authoriza-
tion or the diversion certificate which accompanies
the consignment.
(iii) With regard to transit by land, narcotics stored
in a bonded warehouse, as the other goods, can be
transmitted to another bonded warehouse.
B. Diversion.
1. Are diversion certificates (such as those provided for
by the Geneva Convention) issued for the consignments
diverted from their original destinations?
It is the polioy that the Japanese Government does not
issue such diversion certificates.
IV.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 1.00)
-19-
IV. Internal Control.
A。
Free Ports and Free Zones.
Neither free ports nor free zones exist in Japan.
B. Bonded Warehouses.
1. Are there bonded warehouses in which narcotics may be
stored in the country?
There are bonded warehouses of the customs. Chief
among them are Yokohama, Tokio, Kobe and Osaka.
A person who stores narcotics of foreign consignment
in order to land, reship or transship them on the way
to the definite destination, shall present to the head
of the customs concerned a copy of the export authoriza-
tion or diversion certificate which accompanies the
consignment.
2. What supervision is exercised over narcotics stored in
such warehouses?
The regulations concerning narcotics are applied.
C. Storage.
1.
What requirements are there regarding the manner in
which narcotics in the possession of dealers or manu-
facturers must be stored?
Poisonous drugs(which include morphine and preparations)
and powerful drugs(which include the other narcotic
drugs) shall be kept apart from other drugs;and
poisonous drugs shall be stored in a place provided
with a lock.
D.
Records, Prescriptions and Reports.
1. What records are dealers required to keep?
A dealer in drugs shall keep a book and enter therein,
in

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
in respect of receiving and disposing(including exports and
imports) of narcotic drugs, the names and quantities, the
date, the personal name or trade name, the vocation and the
address of the person from whom he has received the narcotic
drugs or to whom he has disposed of or the address of his
business office; and in the case of (1) morphine, heroin
and cocaine and salts thereof,(2) ecgonine and derivatives
and salts thereof, (3) morphine derivatives and salts, the
particulars inscribed on the vessels or packages and the
serial numbers. This book shall be preserved for five years
from the such date of entry, except(concerning) the narcotic
drugs, which are disposed of in accordance Mith the pre-
soriptions issued by physicians, and the narcotic drugs which
are especially to be designated by the Minister for Home
Affairs.
2. Must prescriptions be filled, and may they be refilled?
A pharmacist prepares medicines in accordance with pre-
seriptions signed by a medical practitioner or stamped
with his seal. When he prepares a medicine containing a
poisonous or powerful drug, he has to stamp his seal on the
prescription and preserve it for three years after the date
on which he has prepared the medicine. This rule, however,
is not applied to a case in which he has not put in the entire
portion of the drug to be used during a certain period as
designated in the prescription. In this case he is re-
quired to enter in the prescription the date on which he
has compounded the medicine and the amount of the drug put
into it and sign his name and stamp his seal under it.
How frequently must reports be made of manufacture, purchases,
3.
sales, and stocks and in what detail?
A

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
A person who has manufactured narcotic drugs shall annually
report by the last day of January of the following year to
the Minister for Home Affairs through the prefectural Governor
of the prefecture, in which he has his factory, the names
and quantities of the drugs he has manufactured by the last
day of December of each year, and the kinds, quantities and
origin of supply of the raw materials.
A person who has imported or exported narcotics, shall report
it within ten days to the Minister for Home Affairs through
the prefectural governor.
4.
If a separate report must be made, or if permission must be
obtained in advance, for each transaction, What is the
procedure?
A person who manufactures the narcotics such as morphine ,
heroin, cocaine etc. or imports or exports all the narcotics,
must apply for permission to the Minister for Home Affairs
through the prefectural governor.
5.
Are reports made to the Department of the Government respon-
sible for issuing import and export certificates?
Reports are made through the prefectural covernor to the
Minister for Home Affairs.
6.
Are all returns of sellers checked against returns of buyers?
No.
E.
Inspection.
1. By what authorities are factories, wholesalers and retailers
inspected?
Especially concerning the narcotics control, the prefectural
governor may cause a sanitary officer or police officer to
examine the certificate of identity or the book of sale, or to
inspect the place in which narootics are manufactured, col-
leoted

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
lected, prepared, stored or sold.
2. If inspection is made by officials of the local Government,
are the results reported to the central Government, and, if so,
to what department and in what detail?
The results are reported, in case and in detail required, to the
central Government and to the other local Government concerned.
3. How frequently are inspections made?
Inspections are made as often as required.
4. If the inspections are not all under one department and if
individual transactions are not reported to a central office, by
what inspection system are books of sellers checked against
books of buyers?
In order to check books of sellers in one prefecture against
books of buyers in another prefecture, close cooperation is
maintained maintained among the prefectural Governors con-
cerned who despatch the inspectors.
F.
Illicit Traffic.
1. If the customs discover an attempt to smuggle à rugs into
or out of the country, do they report immediately to the
authority which issues import and export certificates?
They report immediately to the Ministry of Finance who in
turn reports to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs and
cooperate with the prefectural governors who in turn report
to the Ministry for Home Affairs.
2.
In cases of detection of illicit dealing within the
country, is the central authority immediately notified?
In cases of detection of illicit dealing within the country,
reports are made to the Ministry for Home Affairs who in
turn report to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs.
3.
In cases of smuggling of drugs by post, must the postal
authorities immediately communicate with the central
authority?

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
-25-
authority?
Yes.
4. Does the central authority participate in the criminal pro-
ceedings in narcotios cases?
As in other cases the authorities concerned submit narcotics
cases to the publio prosecutor, but do not participate in the
criminal proceedings.
5. What disposition is made of seized narcotics?
The Law of Penal Procedure stipulates that the prosecutor
disposes of the confiscated substances. Practically the
seized narcotics were sent to the Imperial Hygienic Laboratory
in Tokio who makes use of them.
G. Penalties.
1. What penalties are provided for breach of the narcotics laws
and regulations?
Offenders against the opium monopoly are punishable by fines
and by imprisonment up to two years.
Offenders against the regulations relating to morphine,
cocaine, their salts derivatives, etc., are punishable by
fines and imprisonment up to three months.
V. Miscellaneous.
A. Other Legal or Administrative Measures.
1. Are there any other important legal or administrative
measures controlling the traffic in narcotic drugs?
None.
2.
With what countries, if any, is there an arrangement for
direct exohange between enforcement officers of information
concerning the illicit traffic in narootic drugs?
The Japanese Government concluded with the Government of the
United

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
United States of America in the year 1928 an informal agree-
ment, which is supplemented by another informal arrangement con-
cluded in 1929 between the two countries for the purpose of
aiding each other in the colle ction and communication, by (1)
officials in the Japanese and American diplomatic and consular
services (outside Japanese and American territory) and by (2)
officials in the Japanese and American diplomatic and consular
services and Japanese and American officials charged with the
control of illicit traffic(within Japanese and American terri-
tory), of information facilitating the seizure of narcotics
and the detection and apprehension of those engaged in illicit
traffic therein.
a. What officer is charged with this function?
By the above mentioned agreement it was arranged that the Head of
the Bureau of Treaties of the Foreign Office in Tokio should
directly cooperate with the narcotic commissioner in the
Department of Finance of the United States of America.

MAR 27 1933
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
The Consul at Kobe has informed the Embassy that he
experiences great difficulty in enlisting the assistance of
the local Police authorities in tracing the origin of narcotic
drugs which are found from time to time by the Customs au-
thorities in the United States on vessels ariving from Japan.
This complaint seems to be fairly wide-spread.
In view of these conditions, I suggest that the Customs
MAR 28-1933
authorities
FILED

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
authorities communicate directly with the Embassy when-
ever narcotic drugs are found which appear to be of Japanese
origin. It is suggested that labels taken from the con-
tainers or photographic copies thereof, as well as samples
of the drugs, if possible, be sent with the communication.
The Embassy will then take the matter up with the Japanese
Government with a request for proper investigation. This,
in my judgment, is a more satisfactory method of handling
these cases, as the local authorities are usually unen-
terprising in any matter which may reach beyond their
jurisdiction.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Ottawa, March 27, 1933.
CHLS/GB
SECRET
My dear Mr. Anslinger,
▲ Japanese resident of Vancouver recently received a
letter, dated February 3, 1933, from one K. Yuki of 53 Ichomei,
Kumauchi-dori, Kobe, Japan, from which the following are extracts:
".....and because we learned that there is a very
striet customs inspection at all American and Canadian
ports, we studied and found that the best methods to
overcome this difficulty are as follows:
1. In using members of ships' crews for this
business we will supply them with double cased soles
on their shoes. This method has been adopted in our
business with China and India and proved so suceessful
that not a single case was discovered. By this method,
each person can carry one pound or less on each trip
ashore.
2. In case of shipping as common cargo, it is
sent into the country as canned goods, each can being
perfect in weight, size, etc., to conform with the ship-
ping papers. (It is very dangerous to attempt to use
this method with piekled goods).
With these above methods you will be absolutely free from
any danger of discovery. Kindly consider this matter seriously and
arrange introduetions to us with such members of crews coming into
your club who you believe to be trustworthy, and who would like to
enter into this business.
"Price List as follows:- (Japan)
1. Hydrochloric Acid-Morphine (crystallized like
cotton fibre) - 18 oz. bottle @ 33Y per oz.
2. Heroin - Powdered - 25 oz. bottle @ 32Y per oz.
3. Cocaine - (crystallized in scale form) 25 oz. bottle
@ 32Y per oz.

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
"The above goods are manufactured by permission of the
Minister of Interior and are of best quality for injection or
applied in tobacco. They are all in common use, especially favoured
by Chinese.
"If you do not wish to handle these goods by sailors you
may handle them as common cargo as above mentioned, (an absolutely
perfect method). In this case, if you deposit $1o0.00, we will ship
the goods to you. This is a rather difficult proposition to begin
with, but we have done it previously with good results.
"When you order by cable, please use the cable address,
YUKI,KOBE."
The recipient of this letter has promised to help us in any
way he can in his efforts to learn if anyone else has been approached
in the same manner, although he is extremely anxious that he shall
not be exposed as an informer. He has been instructed to carry on
further correspondenee with Yuki with a view to endeavouring to ascer-
tain something more with regard to these methods of smuggling.
Mr. C. A. Emerick, U. S. Customs Agent of Seattle, was in
Vancouver at the time this information was obtained, and it was
tranamitted to him secretly.
It will be noted that the present value of the Japanese Yen
makes it possible for narcotics to be delivered in Canada for about
$8.00 an ounce, and the price would be even less in your Country.
In view of the long distance between Vancouver and Ottawa,
it is impossible for me to immediately form an estimate, without
a knowledge of the local conditions, as to the extent to which this
information can be disseminated with safety, and I am, therefore,
only, at the moment, writing privately to Perrins and your good self.
I have asked Vancouver to give me their considered opinion as to
whether the exposure of the agent or the further development of the
case would be endangered if the information were confidentially
tranmmitted (a) to the Countries with whom we secretly exchange in-
formation and (b) to the Japanese authorities. If they agree in
regard to (a), I will in due course issue a confidential circular,
but I should imagine that with regard to (b) there will be objeetions.
I would, however, very much appreciate your personal opinion as to
what your owm ideas would be with regard to notifying the Japanese
authorities, under our ordinary International obligations, either now
or at some later date.
The point I am most concerned about is whether our informer

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
is the only person in Vancouver who has received this commmnica-
tion, and as he is continuing the correspondence on our behalf
there might be a serious danger of his identity becoming known
if action were taken along lines which, perhaps, would not be
identical with those which you or I would take in similar
circumstances.
With kind regards.
Sincerely yours,
(signed) C. H. L. Sharman
Chief, Narcotic Division.
H. J. Anslinger, Esq.,
Commissioner of Narcotics,
Treasury Department,
Washington, D. C.

Subject:
Investigation of Cases of
Illicit Traffic in Narcotics
Involving Japan
894.114 NARCOT1CS/73
The appearance on the Pacific Coast of increasing
quantities of morphine apparently of Japanese manufacture
has been causing grave concern to the Bureau of Narcotics
and to the Department of State.
A tabulation is annexed which sets forth the main
particulars of the principal seizures reported to the De-
partment of State since January 1, 1932.
It will be noted that, aside from two instances at
Honolulu involving small quantities of patent medicines,
all of these seizures took place on the Pacific Coast.
The Commissioner of Narcotics informs FE that, as a result
of the large quantities of Japanese morphine now in the
hands of traffickers, the price in the illicit traffic has
FILED
dropped from $200.00 an ounce to $50.00 an ounce. The
22
1933
Commissioner also states that this morphine is being dis-
tributed and peddled to addicts by Japanese subjects.
In each instance of seizure of morphine or of cocaine
whioh appeared to be of Japanese origin, the Commissioner
F1
of

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of Narcotics, acting under the arrangement concluded, at
the request of the Treasury Department, with the Japanese
Government in December, l930, for direct exchange between
the respective heads of narcotics enforcement in the two
countries, has reported the seizure to the Director of the
Bureau of Treaties and Conventions in the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs at Tokyo. In each case, the Commissioner has also
sent either originals or photographs of the labels concerned,
with the usual request for investigation and report. Two
such communications went forward March 28, 1933 and there
has not yet been time for acknowledgment to reach Washington.
Although ample time has elapsed since the despatch of the
Commissioner's communications in the other two cases, all
that has been received is a mere acknowledgment of the letter
in regard to the PRESIDENT MADISON seizure.
When in Geneva last autumn, Mr. Fuller, orally and in-
formally, informed Mr. Kusama, Japanese representative on the
Opium Advisory Committee that increasing quantities of mor-
phine, apparently of Japanese origin, were being found on
the Pacific Coast and were being smuggled, distributed and
peddled by Japanese subjects. He informed Mr. Kusama that
particulars of two seizures had been sent to Tokyo by the
Commissioner of Narcotics for investigation and expressed the
hope that the matter would be taken promptly in hand. Mr.
Kusama said that he would write the Foreign Office urging
prompt

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
prompt action, thorough investigation, and prompt report.
On February 28, 1933, despatoh No. 277, dated Feb-
ruary 1, 1933 (894.114 Narcotics/68) from the American
Ambassador at Tokyo was received at the Department. This
despatoh reads as follows:
"The Consul at Kobe has informed the
Embassy that he experiences great difficulty
in enlisting the assistance of the local Police
authorities in tracing the origin of narootic
drugs which are found from time to time by the
Customs authorities in the United States on
vessels arriving from Japan. This complaint
seems to be fairly wide-spread.
"In view of these conditions, I suggest
that the Customs authorities communicate
directly with the Embassy whenever narcotic
drugs are found which appear to be of Japanese
origin. It is suggested that labels taken from
the containers or photographic copies thereof,
as well as samples of the drugs, if possible,
be sent with the communication. The Embassy
will then take the matter up with the Japanese
Government wi th a request for proper investiga-
tion. This, in my judgment, is a more satis-
factory method of handling these cases, as the
local authorities are usually unenterprising in
any matter which may reach beyond their jurisdic-
tion."
In the latter part of March, 1933, the Department
was informed that the American Treasury Attaché in Japan
was instructed some time last autumn, presumably by the
Commissioner of Customs, to investigate the VENICE MARU
seizure, and possibly the one from the PRESIDENT MADISON,
independently of the direct exchange arrangement provided,
by

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
by agreement between the two governments, to cover such
oases.
It was thus evident that an unfortunate si tuation
had been created, through lack of coordination in the
Treasury Department. The result was that this Department
and the Bureau of Narcotics had not received, from the
Japanese authorities, the cooperation to which the Ameri-
can Government is entitled under the Hague Opium Conven-
tion of l9l2 and the subsidiary agreement, effected by
exchange of notes, to carry out the intent of that Treaty.
The seizures from the PRESIDENT ILADISON and from
the VENICE MARU are bound to come up for discussion at
the meeting of the Opium Advisory Committee in May. When
this occurs, the American representative will be obliged to state
that no report has been received from the Japanese Govern-
ment on either case. The Japanese reply will probably
place the American Government in the same unenviable posi-
tion in which the French placed us last year. It will be
recalled that the French representative at that time stated
that, although a regular arrangement was in effect with the
American Government specifying the way in which such cases
should be handled, the French authorities never knew where
they stood in American cases because they would be addressed,
in a single case, by several American Government agencies
acting independently of each other, including the Commissioner
of

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
at Washington,
of Customs, American Treasury Attachés in France and
Treasury officials in New York. In the very case in
whioh the American representatives (in cooperation with
the British, Indian, Egyptian, Dutch and German represen-
tatives) had planned to point out lack of French coopera-
tion, the French read a laudatory letter of appreciation
(entirely undeserved ) from the American Commissioner of
at Washington,
Customs, who, so far as FE is informed, is not authorized
to correspond with the French narcotic authorities.
To meet the situation temporarily, the following
telegraphio instruction was sent (in confidential code)
to the American Embassy at Tokyo, Match 27, 1933:
"Confidential.
68
"Embassy's despatoh No.277, February 1.
"One. Increasing amounts of morphine
apparently of Japanese manufacture appearing
in the illicit traffic on the Pacific Coast
are causing grave concern.
"Two. Under arrangement for direct exchange
of information concerning illicit traffic, the
Commissioner of Narcotics, on July 25, 1932, sent
to the Director of the Bureau of Treaties at
Tokyo particulars of the seizure on July 7 from
the Steamship VENICE MARU at Seattle of thirteen
pounds of morphine bearing Japanese labels. On
October 3, 1932, he sent additional data including
photograph of shipping tag concerned. No acknowledg-
ment of these communications has been received.
"Three

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
"Three. The Department is now informed
that the Treasury Attaché in Japan was in-
structed in July last by another bureau of
the Treasury Department to investigate this
case independently of the direct exchange pro-
vided for such cases, which was outlined in
the Embassy's despatch No. 94, dated December 29,
1930. No information has been received as to
the result of any investigation made by the
Treasury Attaché. The Department is apprehensive
lest this duplication has interfered with the ef-
feotive handling of the case.
"Four. Without, repeat,without, approaching
the Japanese authorities, please ascertain and
report by telegraph what action, if any, the
Treasury Attaché has taken in this case, with what
result and whether he has taken action in any sub-
sequent cases of seizure of narcotics in the United
States.
"Five. Instruct all consular officers in
Japan to refer to the Embassy requests of Collectors
of Customs for investigation of narcotics seizures.
The Embassy should in such cases, within its dis-
cretion, ask the Japanese correspondent under the
direct exchange of information to investigate and
to send the data directly to the Commissioner of
Narcotics. The Department should be informed of
each such case. Meanwhile, the Department will
take up with the Treasury Department the question
of having Collectors of Customs instructed to
submit their requests for investigations through
the Commissioner of Narcotics under the direct
exchange arrangement referred to above. BILTREASRY."
20
The Ambassador's reply, dated March 30, 1933, is
quoted below:
"Department's 20, March 27, 5 p.m., narcotics.
"Treasury Attaché reports that he has made
only two requests for investigation during the
past year. Following are the two: Case of the
VENICE MARU on which narcotics were reported to have
been

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
been seized in Seattle on July 7, and the
other the PRESIDENT MADISON on which narco tics
were found on August 7. In both instances the
request for an investigation was made through
the Amerioan Consul at Kobe who addressed the
local authorities at Osaka without result. I
am instructing all consular officers to refer
future cases to the Embassy.
"In view of the lack of results in the
cases which have been referred to the Japanese
Government, I think it would be advisable to
approach the Foreign Office and to ask the Foreign
Minister to look into the question. Apparently
these investigations are handled by subordinates
without muoh authority. A manifestation of inter-
est on the part of those higher up might be of
benefit. Please instruct."
On April 14, 1933, Mr. Fuller telephoned to Mr. Shamhart
in the Bureau of Customs, to inquire whether the Treasury
Attaché had ever reported in regard to a seizure of Japanese
heroin which took place in January, 193l. Mr. Shamhart
loaned to FE certain papers from the Bureau of Customs files,
photostat copies of two of which are annexed. The Treasury
Attachés letter of January 19, 1933, shows the efforts made
by him to obtain information in the PRESIDENT MADISON and
VENICE MARU cases. Al though the Treasury Department has had
this information for over two months, the Department of State
has not hitherto been informed in the premises. Agent Hanks'
letter of September 7, 1932, contains information which should
have been transmitted to this Department, but, apparently
was

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
was not turned over to the Commissioner of Narcotics,
who is liaison with the Department of State in matters
concerning narcotic drugs.
Whatever may be the reasons (and they are several),
it is evident that the American Government is not receiving
the cooperation which it should receive from the Japanese
Government in narcotics cases. It also looks as though our
own bureaux and departments were not adequately cooperating.
In view of existing conditions, it is submitted that
it would be well to arrange to have all requests for in-
vestigation of narcotics cases from now on, for a while
at least, placed before the Japanese Foreign Office by
the American Embassy at Tokyo.
A draft is annexed of a letter to the Treasury De-
partment suggesting this procedure and requesting the
Secretary of the Treasury to instruct the Commissioner of
Narcotics accordingly and to direct the Treasury Attaché
at Kobe to approach the Japanese authorities in narco tics
cases only through the American Embassy at Tokyo.
When the Treasury Department's agreement is received,
the American Ambassador at Tokyo can be instructed accord-
ingly by mail. At the same time, a telegraphic instruction
can be sent to the Ambassador stating that the Treasury
Attaché is being instructed to take up narcotics cases
through the Embassy only.
In

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.97)
-9-
In the meantime, it is suggested that the attached
telegram be sent to the American Consul General at Tokyo
directing him to instruct all consular officers in Japan
to send to the Department, under confidential cover, copies
of all correspondence which they address to the Treasury
Attachés in Japan in regard to narcotics. A similar ar-
rangement is now in effect in France and in Hong Kong.
It works well and consideration is being given to estab-
lishing such a rule throughout the service.
StuaJ. Fuller.

U. S. NARCOTIC SERVICE
Los Angeles, California
April 14, 1955
Mr. H. J. Anslinger
Commissioner of Narcotics
Washington, D. C.
Personal and Confidential
Dear Mr. Anslinger:
Reference is made to Progress Reports submitted by
me during the past several months, practically all of which
contained suggestions relative to the reported activity of
Japanese smmgglers and distributors on the Pacific Coast which
resulted in a lowering of prices and obviously presenting a
problem most diffioult to cope with.
As I recall, this matter was discussed with you
orally on your visit to the Coast last year, since which time
it has constantly become more apparent that morphine in large
quantities was being imported from Japan by Japanese.
The case just reported by me from Los Angeles in-
volving three Japanese defendants and identified as Cal-
1517 is clearly evidential of the extent of this traffie and
at the same time an investigation was concluded in Seattle,
as well as a large seizure of morphine being made by State
traffic officers in Oregon, all of which involved Japanese.
I have in mind also Colonel Sharman's confidential letter
to you, a copy of which was forwarded me, touching on the
same subjest. There seems to be no limit to the extent of
activities by these people and source of supply in Japan and
they are offering this contraband in quantities which in my
opinion will soon approach proportions of the former European
ring.
The principal defendant in Case Cal-1517 whose name
is Mayeda and whose cable registry was reported to you
supplementally as Kojitaka, undoubtedly has been one of the
principal importers in this section, and it is obvious that
between the arrests in Seattle, Portland and Los Angeles all
involving morphine in the hands of and imported by Japanese,
presents evidencs of sufficient value to form the basis of a

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
gemeral investigation by and with Japanese authorities if
they are recipient. The supplemental report to you in
commection with Case Cal-l5l7 contains a true copy of a
eablekrem directed to a person in Osaka, Japan, whose cable
registry is indicated as Kawatatsu and prior to the arrest
of the three defendants in the Los Angeles case at least one
had told informer Louie that their Japanese connection was
a menufacturer who would only do business with three Japanese
on the Pacific Coast of the United States.
I presume the Division of Foreign Control is co-
ordinating all of these matters and in view of the fact that
w are gradually breaking in to the organization and are in
possession of sufficient evidence to indicate that no diffi-
culty is being encountered by the importers in getting this
contraband through Customs, that the various Collectors at
Paeific coast ports should have the situation strongly
brought to their attention.
If you have any suggestions or instructions, I would
highly appreciate being advised.
Yours very truly,
(signed) Harry D. Smith
District Supervisor
Dist. #15.

agencies actine independently of sach other, including the
Commissioner of Customs, American Treasury Attachés in
France and Treasury officials in New York. In disoussing
the very case in whioh the American representatives (in co-
operation with the British, Indian, Egyptian, Dutch and
German representatives) had planned at that meeting to point
out lack of effective French cooperation, the Frenoh repre-
sentative read a laudatory letter of appreciation from the
American Commissioner of Customs, who, so far as this De-
partment is informed, is not authorized to correspond
directly with the French authorities in charge of narootics
administration.
It is feared that a repetition of this embarrassing
situation can not te avoided at the coming session of the
Opium Advisory Committee and that the Japanese representa-
tive on that Committee, following the preoedent set by the
Frenoh

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Freneh representative, will endeavor to justify the lack
of Japanese cooperation on similar grounds.
While that can not now be helped, it is considered
important to make arrangements for future handling of
requests to the Japanese Government to investigate seizures
of narcotio drugs which appear to be of Japanese origin
of a kind that will prevent such duplication and conse-
quent embarrassment to the Americen Government.
The Department las directed the American Ambassador
at Tokyo to instruct all consular officers in Japan to re-
fer to the American Embassy at Tokyo all requests of Col-
lectors of Customs for investigation of narcotics seizures.
The Embassy has also been direoted to inform this Depart-
ment of each such case and has been authorized, wi thin its
disoretion, to ask the Japanese correspondent under the
established arrangement for direot exohange of information
to investigate suoh cases and to send the data directly to
the Commissioner of Narcotics.
For many reasons, in view of conditions existing et
the present time, this Department considers it advisable
that all requests to the Japanese Government for the in-
vestigation of cases involving narcotic drugs should, for
the time being. be presented to the Japanese Foreign Office
by the American Embassy at Tokyo.
