TREASURY DEPARTMENT
SPECIAL AGENCY SERVICE
U. S. CUSTOMS
SEATTLE, WASH.
September 7, 1932.
Commissioner of Customs,
(Investigative Unit),
Treasury Department,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
This communication relates to the smuggling
of so-called dangerous drugs (white stuff) from Japan
to the United States. Apparently the shipment of
drugs from Japan to the United States is on the
increase, especially since the demoralizing effect
the recent seizures in New York have had on the ship-
ment of illicit drugs from Europe.
At a recent conference in San Francisco,
California, with Mr. Anslinger, Commissioner of the
Bureau of Narcotics, he stated it was very important
to trace the illicit shipments to their manufacturing
source, as it might be possible to cause the closing
down of the factory. As this report is relevant to
such an endeavor, it is respectfully requested that a
copy of this report be furnished Mr. Anslinger.
On January 27, 1931, the office of the Customs
Agent in Seattle, Washington, made e seizure of 24 oz.
of Heroin. The contraband was in its original contain-
or with the following inscription on the wrapper -
"Japan Pharmaceutical Establishment,
Osaka, Japan."
Martin G. Scott, U. S. Treasury Attache at Kobe,
Japan, upon a request from this office, made an investi-
gation of this matter in Japan and under dates of April
2nd and l6th, 1931, reported that since the passage of
a new narcotic law dated June 7, 1930, only one fim in Osaka was
licensed to engage in the production of narcotics, namely -
"Da1 Nihon Seiyaku Kabushiki Kaisha,
30 Dosho-machi, 3-chome,
Higashi-ka, Osaka."

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the products of this firm bear the same inscription as on the
Azed heroin, namely - "Japan Pharmaceutical Establishment"; that
in addition, each container of narcotics disposed of by the company
bears on the outside wrapper and on the container itself, a amall
rectangular sticker on which are written identification numbers in
code; that a careful check was kept of these numbers as they indi-
cate the date of sale and the purchaser of the goods; that these
numbers are subject to check by the Police at all times; that the
sale of narcotics by this firm is supposed to be under st#tet
government supervision and only made to drug brokers who have been
certified by the government and possess a license to deal in narcotics;
that such brokers are allowed to sell only to hospitals, doctors,
licensed pharmacists, etc., who in turn must be licensed to purchase
narcotos.
Mr. Scott continued, that the Police authorities in Osaka
had promised to conduct a thorough investigation in the matter; that
they requested for this purpose, photographic copies of all labels,
otc. found on seizures in the United States; that the Japanese police
appeared to be suspicious of the Japan Pharmaceutical Chemical Labora-
tory. 390, Okayama-cho, Higashi-ku, Osaka, as this firm had in the
past been guilty of attempting to imitate the products and marks of
the Dai Nihon Company; that the Japan Pharmaceutical Chemical Labora-
tory was not supposed to manufacture narcotics since the passage of
the act of June 7. 1930.
Mr. Scott further stated uhat a visit was made by himself
to the Japan Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd., 1 of 34, Koraibashi-dzume,
Higashi-ku, Osaka, where the premises appeared to be occupied solely
by the Obayashi Note-Book Manufacturing Company, though in addition
to the sign-board of the Obayashi Company there was another smaller
one with the inscription "Nippon Seiyaku Kabushiki Kaisha", meaning
the Japan Pharmaceutical Co.. Ltd.; that he was advised that the
building had not been used by the Pharmaceutical Company for over two
years, though their sign was left standing as a matter of courtesy;
that the said Pharmaceutical Company had been organized for the
manufacture of patent medicines some five or six years ago but had
been bankrupt for two years; that since the firms bankruptcy a Mr.
Takeo Tanaka had had a desk in the building to the beginning of 1930,
that however, such desk had been removed and Mr. Tanaka was now
employed by the Lap Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd., Kuize, near Amagasaki
City (Between Kobe and Osaka).
Mr. Scott states that he also visited the Japan Pharmaceut-
ical Chemical Laboratory at 390, Okayama-cho, Higashi-ku, Osaka, where
he interviewed Mr. Tsunekichi Ito,younger brother of the proprietor;
that Mr. Ito advised that they manufactured varions kinds of drugs and
patent medicines for sale exclusively in Japan, and that so far as they
knew none of their drugs were ever sold or shipped to the United States.

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
On July 7, 1932, Customs officers in Seattle, Washington,
ized (ex-Japanese SS. Venice Maru) sixty tins of morphine, each tin
containing 25 ounces net, and each tin measuring 6-7/8" x 6-7/8" x 8-3/8".
Each tin was marked with the capital letter "p" in a circle, with
"Seiyaku Dai Nippon" surrounding the "p" in a circle. The tins were
sealed with a capital "p" in a circle (Japan Pharmaceutical Establishment).
Translation of the Japanese label which was pasted on each tin reveals
the address as Dosho-machi, Osaka, Japan.
One white man and three Japanese were apprehended with this
seizure, one of the Japanese defendants, who was chief stewaru on the SS
Venice Maru, was named Tadauki Ito (note - the same family name as one of
the officials of the Japan Pharmaceutical Chemical Laboratory).
Fifty-eight tins of this seizure were forwarded to the
Commissioner af Narcotics from Seattle on August 31, 1932, on which the
original wrappers are intact. An inspection of the two tins held in
Seattle reveals that rectangular stickers, referred to in paragraph four
of this report, had been placed on the outside wrappers but had been
defaced to such an extent that no numbers are decipherable. Accordingly
the following wire was dispatched this date to the Bureau:
"Please request Narcotic Bureau check fifty eight cans of morphine
shipped from Collector Seattle August thirty first to Narcotic
Commissioner for identifying numbers in rectangular square below
inscription muriate of morphia P J four and advise whether or not
decipherable Stop this number according to Japanese regulations
identifie purehaser from factory Stop numbers on two cans held
here defaced and undecipherable. Signed Hanks"
It is believed that a microscopic inspection may assist in deter-
mining the identifying numbers.
In view of the fact that the marks on the packages so closely
follow the description of the product of the - "Dai Nihon Seiyaku Kabushiki
Kaisha". also that rectamgular stickers had been originally placed on the
packages, indicates that the seizure originated at this factory. However,
since the Japanese Police suspect the Japan Pharmaceutical Chemical
Laboratory of imitating the products and marks of the Dai Nihon Seiyaku
Kabushiki Kaisha", it may be possible that this firm is manufacturing
narcotics in violation of Japanese regulations.
Photographs of the labels and markings on the two cans
held in Seattle, are being forwarded to the Treasury Attache at Kobe,
Japan, for checking with the Osaka Police.
An investigation is now being conducted by this office
of the United States end of this ring, which to date shows considerable

=== Page 134 === (confidence 1.00)
romise. The Bureau will be kept advised of all further developments.
Respectfully,
(Signed) Melvin L. Hmks
MELVIN L. HANKS,
Customs Agent at Large.
Respectfully forwarded,
Approved:
(Signed) A. B. Hamer
A. B. HAMER,
Actg. Supervising Customs Agent.

UNITED STATES TREASURY DEPARTMENT
AMERICAN CONSULATE
KOBE, JAPAN
January 19, 1933.
The Commissioner of Customs,
Investigative Unit,
Treasury Department,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
Reference is respectfully made to letter of September ?,
1952, - file 14-46, - from Customs Agent at Large Melvin L.
Hanks, Seattle, to the Commissioner of Customs, re the seizure
on the Japanese steamship Venice Maru of sixty tins of morphine,
each tin containing 25 ounces net, and each measuring 6-7/8"
I 8-3/8", all marked and stated to be manufactured by the Japan
Pharmaceutical Establishment, Osaka. Mr. Hanks advises:
"Photographs of the labels and markings on the
two cans held in Seattle, are being forwarded
to the Treasury Attache at Kobe, Japan, for
checking with the Osaka Police."
Prior to the receipt of this canmunication, we were requested
by Mr. E. L. Matteson, General Agent of the Dollar Steamship Line,
Kobe, to assist in tracing the sale of fourteen bottles of morphine
hydrochloride found on the President Madison on her arrival at
Seattle August 9, l932. Labels were forwarded which were removed from
the seized bottles. A translation of these labels showed the morphine
to have been made by the Osaka Hygienic Labaratory. Osaka. The labels
also bore the serial number prescribed by the Japanese narcotic law.
The matter thus seemed quite easy to trace.
On October 8, 1932, through the American Consul, Kobe, request
was made of the Osaka police to trace this transaction. Request on
the letter of Customs Agent Hanks was withheld, pending a reply to this
first request.
In spite of the fact that strict Japanese supervision, as provided
in the Japanese Narcotic Law, is exercised by the police, rendering
the tracing of this transaction a routine matter only, nothing was
done by the police. The Consul's letter was not even replied to.

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
On January 6, 1933, the writer then requested Consul Howard
Donovan to bring to the attention of the Osaka police the seizure
on the Venice Maru.
On January 10, 1933, Vice Consul Edmund J. Dorsz visited Osaka
and conferred with Mr. S. Mivata and Mr. Mori, Chief and Vice Chief,
respectively, of the Osaka Prefectural Police. A copy of Mr. Dorsz's
report of this interview is attached hereto.
Regarding the seizure on the President Madison, the police ad-
vised that the morphine was manufactured by the "Osaka Hygienic
Laboratory" which information, of course, we already possessed. They
also state that the sale "had been accounted for to the satisfaction
of the police by the persons to whom they were sold." Apparently the
fact that certain of these bottles were seized by United States Customs
authorities does not detract from the satisfaction of the Osaka police
as regards the integrity of the parties handling this narcotic.
As regards the seizure on the Venice Maru, the police advised
that they could do nothing without a sample of the morphine or a
label. The situation seems to be that unless labels are furnished
the police can do nothing, and if they are furnished they only serve
to confirm the police in their satisfaction of the integrity of the
parties concerned.
As a matter of fact the labels referred to by Customs Agent
Hanks, in his letter of September 7th, were not received by this
office. As far as securing results goes, it doesn't make a particle
of difference whether or not they were forwarded. The statements
of the Osaka police are all buncombe. The Japanese narcotic laws are
very strict in requiring the registration of all particulars
accompanying all narcotic sales. Add to that the fact that the
Japanese police exercise very strict supervision over every action
of a Japanese subject anyway. Add to that the fact that in one
instance they had actual labels to work on, and in the other the name
of the manufacturer, it will be seen how absurd their explanation
that they were unable to do anything.
I am attaching herewith copy of letter from Consul Howard
Donovan, dated January 12, 1933. It is not ed that Mr. Donovan
states that "In future such cases will be referred the Embassy."
Mr. Donovan believes that nothing is to be gained under present
conditions by negotiating with the Osaka police authorities. In
this opinion, this office heartily concurs.
On previous occasinns, this office had had occasion to solicit
the assistance of the Osaka police in narcotic matters. The sum

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
total of their assistance to date has been a perfect blank, and may
be expeeted to be so unless or until some pressure may be brought upon
these gentlemen to do something besides furnish character certificates
for parties engaged in the narcotic traffic. This pressure can only
be exerted diplomatically through the State Department. It is of
course not within the purview of this office to suggest what should
be done.
It is the opinion of this office, however, that the situation
may be more serious than it appears. Unquestionably, Osaka is an
impertant center for the manufacture and sale of narcotics. Due to
the lack of any reliable published official infomation, it is
difficult to say just how important it is. Now, if the police of this
important narcotic center are disposed to look with a tolerent eye,
if it be no worse than that, on matters as they exist, it requires
no stretch of the imagination to visualize Osaka as an important
problem in the smuggling of narcotics into the United States.
In conclusion, it is our opinion that it is uodless to
forward this office any requests for information on this subject.
This office is in no position to accomplish any unoer-cover work.
With the strict police surveillance that exists, any attempt to do
so would only have the most unpleasant reactions. We can therefore
only rely on the assistance of the local police. As long as this
is refused, we are helpless.
Respectfully,
(Signed) Martin G. Scott
Treasury Attache.
Copies:
Department (2)
Customs Agent Hanks (1)

CONFIDENTIAL
American Consulate, Kobe, Japan, January 12, 1933.
Martin G. Scott, Esquire,
United States Treasury Attache,
Kobe, Japan.
Sir:
I wish to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of January
6, 195s, regarding sixty tins of morphine seized at Seattle on the
Japanese S.S. VENICE MARU on July 7, 1932. Reference is also made to
your memorandum of October 8, l932, regarding fourteen bottles of morphine
found on board the American S.S. PRESIDENT MADISON at Seattle on August
9, 1952.
Your attention is invited to the attached copy of a memorandum
submitted by Vice Consul Dorsz which summaries the results of his inter-
view on the subject with the Osaka police. Both cases have, and will
continue to be, dealt with by the Osaka police in their usual farcial
manner. The police could, if they wished, ascertain very easily who
sold the morphine to the person or persons on the Venice Maru and on
the President Madison but it is obvious that they will not make any effort
to do so. In future such cases will be referred to the Embassy as
suggested by the Ambassador in his letter of December 28, 1932.
A copy of this letter, with enclosures, vill be forwarded to the
American Consul General at Tokyo for transmission to the Embassy at
his discretion.
Respectfully yours,
(Signed) Howard Donovan
American Consul.

Kobe, Japan, January 11, 1933.
andum of interview between Vice Consul Dorss and Mr. S. Miyata and
o1, Chief and Vice Chief, respectively, of the Osaka Prefectural
mont on January 10. 1933. Mr. Suzuki of the Consulate acted as
rpreter.
Mr. Miyata appeared to understand English perfectly but he apparently
preforred reply in Japanese thus requiring the services of Mr. Suzuki in
translating which at best is very ineffective.
From the beginning of the interview it appeared to me that little
success would be had in obtaining information relating to the possible
apprehension of the persons involved in the shipment of the l4 bottles of
morphine hydrochloride which were found on the S. S. PRESIDENT MADISON
when it docked at Seattle, Washington, on August 9, l932.
Mr. Miyata informed me that the Consulate's letters of October 15,
November 12, and December 9, 1932, had not been answered because the Japanese
police had not completed their investigation, and expressed his regret in the
matter by stating that the year end activities had kept the police busy with
other poobloms.
He stated that the police had discovered that 50 bottles of Morphine
Hydrochloride having the same license number as that on the label forwarded
on Oetober 15, 1oe, by this Consulate to the Osaka Prefectural Government
had been sold in the City of Osake and that all 50 bottles had been accounted
for to the satisfaction of the police by the persons to whom they were sold.
He continued by stating that the police had not completed their investigation
in regard to the number of bottles heving the same license number which had
been sold outside of Osaka Prefecture. When such investigation is completed,
he promised to inform this Consulate of the results.
When requested for information regarding the number of bottles manufactured
under the same number as that of the label and for other information concerning
the amount of narcotics which firms having licenses could manufacture yearly,
he evaded my questions by repeating his former statements regarding the 50
bottles discovered by the police in Osaka Prefecture.
With regard to the 60 tins of morphine found on the VENICE MARU when it
arrived at Seattle, Washington, on July 7, 1932, Mr. Miyata stated that the
police could not effectively continue an investigation of this case with the
view of apprehending the persons involved in the smuggling of such narcotics
unless either some of the morphine or a label from one of the tins were
forwarded to the Osaka Prefectural Government.
When it became obvious that the interview was unsuccessful, I let Mr.
Miyata and Mr. Mori continue their remarks until they decided to end the
disoussion. I tmanked them, together and separately, for their courtesy in

anting me the interview and for the assistance which the police had
ven in the matter.

mining the identifying numbers.

follow the description of the product of the - "Dai Nihon Seiyaku Kabushiki

packages, indicates that the seizure originated at this factory. However,
since the Japanese Police suspect the Japan Pharmaceutical Chemical
Laboretory of imitating the products and marks of the Dai Nihon Seiyaku
Kabushiki Kaisha", it may be possible that this firm is manufacturing
narcoties in violation of Japanese regulations.
Photographs of the labels and markings on the two cans

UNTTED STATES TREASURY DEPARTMENT
AMERICAN CONSULATE
KOBE, JAPAN
January 19, 1933.
The Commissioner of Customs,
Investigative Unit,
Treasury Department,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
Reference is respectfully made to letter of September 7,
1932, - file 14-46, - from Customs Agent at Large Melvin L.
Hanks, Seattle, to the Commissioner of Customs, re the seizure
on the Japanese steamship Venice Maru of sixty tins of morphine,
each tin containing 25 ounces net, and each measuring 6-7/8"
x 8-3/8", all marked and stated to be manufactured by the Japan
Pharmaceutioal Establishment, Osaka. Mr. Hanks advises:
"Photographs of the labels and markings on the
two cans held in Seattle, are being forwarded
to the Treasury Attache at Kobe, Japan, for
checking with the Osaka Police."
Prior to the receipt of this canmunication, we were requested
by Mr. E. L. Matteson, General Agent of the Dollar Steamship Line,
Kobe, to assist in tracing the sale of fourteen bottles of morphine
hydrochloride found on the President Madison on her arrival at
Seattle August 9, l932. Labels were forwarded which were removed from
the seized bottles. A translation of these labels showed the morphine
to have been made by the Osaka Hygienic Labaratory, Osaka. The labels
also bore the serial number prescribed by the Japanese narcotic law.
The matter thus seemed quite easy to trace.
On October 8, 1932, through the American Consul, Kobe, request
was made of the Osaka police to trace this transaction. Request on
the letter af Customs Agent Hanks was withheld, pending a reply to this
first request.
In spite of the fact that strict Japanese supervision, as provided
in the Japanese Narcotic Law, is exercised by the police, rendering
the tracing of this transaction a routine matter only, nothing was
done by the police. The Consul's letter was not even replied to.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
On January 6, 1933, the writer then requested Consul Howard
novan to bring to the attention of the Osaka police the seizure
on the Venice Maru.
On January 10, 1933, Vice Consul Edmund J. Dorsz visited Osaka
and conferred with Mr. S. Mivata and Mr. Morl, Chief and Vice Chief,
respectively, of the Osaka Prefectural Police. A copy of Mr. Dorsz's
report of this interview is attached herato.
Regarding the seizure on the President Madison, the police ad-
vised that the morphine was manufactured by the "Osaka Hygienic
Laboratory" which information, of course, we already possessed. They
also state that the sale "had been accounted for to the satisfaction
of the police by the persons to whom they were sold." Apparently the
fact that certain of these bottles were seized by United States Customs
authorities does not detract from the satisfaction of the Osaka police
as regards the integrity of the parties handling this narcotic.
As regards the seizure on the Venice Maru, the police advised
that they could do nothing without a sample of the morphine or a
label. The situation seems to be that unless labels are furnished
the police can do nothing, and if they are furnished they only serve
to confirm the police in their satisfaction of the integrity of the
partiesconcerned.
As a matter of fact the labels referred to by Customs Agent
Hanks, in his letter of September 7th, were not received by this
office. As far as securing results goes, it doesn't make a particle
of difference whether or not they were forwarded. The statements
of the Osake police are all buncombe. The Japanese narcotic laws are
very strict in requiring the registration of all particulars
accompanying all narcotic sales. Add to that the fact that the
Japanese police exercise very strict supervision over every action
of a Japanese subject anywey. Add to that the fact that in one
instance they had actual labels to work on, and in the other the name
of the manufacturer, it will be seet how absurd their explanation
that they were unable to do anything.
I am attaching herewith copy of letter from Consul Howard
Donovan, dated January 12, 1933. It is not ed that Mr. Donovan
states that "In future such cases will be referred the Embassy."
Mr. Donovan believes that nothine is to be gained under present
conditions by negotiating with the Osaka police authorities. In
this opinion, this office heartily concurs.
On previous occasinns, this office had had occasion to solicit
the assistance of the Osaka police in narcotic matters. The sum

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
total of their assistance to date has been a perfect blank, and may
be expected to be so unless or until some pressure may be brought upon
these gentlemen to do something besides furnish character certificates
for parties engaged in the narcotic traffic. This pressure can only
be exerted diplomatically through the State Department. It is of
course not within the purview of this office to suggest what should
be done.
It is the opinion of this office, however, that the situation
may be more serious than it appears. Unquestionably, Osaka is an
important center for the manufacture and sale of narcotics. Due to
the lack of eny reliable published official infomation, it is
difficult to say just how important it is. Now, if the police of this
important narcotic center are disposed to look with a tolerent eye,
if it be no worse than that, on matters as they exist, it requires
no stretch of the imagination to visualize Osaka as an important
problem in the smuggling of narcotics into the United States.
In conclusion, it is our opinion that it is useless to
forward this office any requests for information on this subject.
This office is in no position to accomplish any under-cover work.
With the strict police surveillance that exists, any attempt to do
so would only have the most unpleasant reactions. We can therefore
only rely on the assistance of the local police. As long as this
is refused, we are helpless.
Respectfully,
(Signed) Martin G. Scott
Treasury Attache.
Copies:
Department (2)
Customs Agent Hanks (1)

The Secretary of State.
Sir:
Receipt is acknowledged of your confidential letter of
April 18, 1933, your file FE 894.114 Narcotics/, regarding investiga-
tions in Japan of matters relating to the smuggling into the United
States of narcotic drugs of Japanese manufacture.
You state that, for many reasons, in view of conditions
existing at the present time, your Department considers it advisable
that all requests to the Japanese Government for the investigation of
cases involving narcotic drugs should, for the time being, be presented
to the Japanese Foreign Office by the American Embassy at Tokyo.
I have to advise that, while this Department has ample
facilities and qualified personnel in the office of the Treasury Attache
at Kobe to conduct such investigations, in view of your statement that
conditions existing at the present time make it advisable that requests
to the Japanese Government for narcotic investigations should be presented
to the Japanese Foreign Office by the American Embassy at Tokyo, this
Department is willing to acquiesce, for the time being, in the plan you
suggest and is appropriately instructing its officials.
By direction of the Secretary:
Respectfully,

The accused persons(Tak@naga, Tanagida, Morita
and Ai), acting in collusion, planned to export,

one Kosaku Tsujido 18 bottles of cocaine, each con-

January 18th) to Morita, a stoker on board the N.Y.K.
S.S. "Asama Mary*. Morita secretly put them on
board this steamer which left Kobe on that day, and
landed them at San Francisco on February 3rd.

from one Watanabe 10 bottles of cocaine, each

examined them with the result that they confessed

Komataro Matsuzaki, born on April 6th, 1904;

(2) The offences committed.
The two accused persons jointly planned to export
morphine without the requisite permission of the
Minister for Home Affairs, taking advantage of the
fact that Matsuzaki was serving as cook's assistant
on board the N.Y.K. S.S. "Tatsuta Maru" plying between
Japan ports and Los Angeles. On May 1st, 1933, Nishi-
jima at his house delivered one pound of morphine to
Matsuzaki who with this morphine embarked on board the
S.S."Tatsuta Maru", then at anchor in Kobe, the same
evening. On the following day the steamer left Kobe
for Los Angeles where she arrived on the 20th, and
thus the morphine was smuggled.
(3)
How the offences were discovered.
When a member of the Kobe Water Police visited
the S.S. "Tatsuta Maru" on her arrival in Kobe on
June 10th, 1933, he learned that Matsuzaki had
brought a considerable amount of money from Los
Angeles, and heard a rumour that he was implicated in
asmuggling case. The policeman accordingly ex-
amined him with the result that he confessed to the
above-mentioned offences.

WASHINGTON
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
instruction of September 25, 1933 (File No. 894.1l4
Narcotics), requesting full details of the trial and
6
2
conviction of a gang of narcotics smugglers who on
August 15, 1933 were reported to have been fined
Yen 5,000,000 by Judge Onishi of the Osaka Court of
Appeals.
Inquiry was made of Judge Onishi as to the case
in question. Mr. Onishi stated that under the law he
himself was unable to discuss matters pertaining to a
trial before him, but that Mr. Saburo Tanida, President
of the Osaka Court of Appeals, would be free to supply
the desired information. This he very effectively did
NOV 83 1933
by presenting the Consulate with a transcript of the
FILED
court record of the case in question.
The staff of this office is at present not adequate
to permit of a complete translation of the Japanese text
of

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of the testimony and sentence. The essential details
have, however, been ascertained as set forth below.
The complete text in katakana is transmitted herewith
as an enelosure to this despatch. The salient points
of the case may be summarized as follows:
1. The Case.
Appeal from the Osaka District Court to the Osaka
Court of Appeals against conviction by the court of
first instance on the charge of smuggling narcotics in
violation of the Narcotics and Customs laws of Japan.
2. Names of the Convicted Smugglers.
Keiji Kitada, aged 51, a contractor residing in
Kohama-mura, Kawabe-gun, Hyogo-ken.
Mantaro Asada, aged 36, a druggist residing at
11 Awaza 3-bancho, Nishi-ku, Osaka.
Kanichi Shimamura, aged 52, resident of Shioya,
Tarumi-cho, Akashi-shi, Hyogo-ken.
844c,114 nax/27
Shigeichi Ihara, aged 30, resident of Sonoda-
mura, Kawabe-gun, Hyogo-ken.
Gihei Miyamoto, aged 46, a resident of Aoki-cho,
Kanagawa-ku, Yokohama-shi.
Minezo Yajima, aged 62, a druggist residing at
167l Aza Kishimoto, Hirano, Mikage-cho,
Muko-gun, Hyogo-ken.
3. The Nature of the Drugs Smuggled.
Benzol Morphine.
Benzil Morphine.
4. The Sources of the Drugs.
Leopold Mager Company of Hamburg, Germany, supplied
2,635 kilogrammes of Benzol Morphine
1,1l5 kilogrammes of Benzil Morphine.
E. Paul Company of Hamburg, Germany, supplied
200 kilogrammes of Benzol Morphine
200 kilogrammes of Benzil Morphine.
Rudowitchgrafe (translation dubious) Company of
Hamburg, Germany, supplied
100 kilogrammes of Benzil Morphine.
5.

=== Page 187 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
5. The Disposition of the Narcotics.
The bulk of the narcotics itemized in the preceding
paragraph had been disposed of by the gang before their
apprehension by the police. The drugs found by the
authorities amounted to 383,120.4 grams of Benzil
Morphine. Kitada, Shimamura, Ihara and Miyamoto were
the actual smugglers, disposing of their drugs through
Asada and Yajima, who owned a drug store in Osaka. The
police found a list kept by Asada of the persons to whom
he disposed of the drugs. Both partners were aware that
the narcotics they sold had been smuggled. The transcript
of the court record indicates that Asada and Yajima
disposed of the drugs to Shintaro Inouye and Shinichi
Masuda who were evidently peddlers to addicts. The
addresses and nominal occupations of these two are not
shown in the record of the appeal.
6. Appeal from the Decision.
Kitada, Shimamura, Ihara and Yajima have appealed
the decision of the Osaka Court of Appeals to the
Supreme Court at Tokyo. The Consulate has endeavored
to ascertain from the officials of the Osaka tribunal
when the case will be heard before the Supreme Court,
but they state that they have no information on the
subject. The matter doubtless depends on how full is
the docket of the Supreme Court. The Consulate General
at Tokyo can in all probability ascertain the approximate
date of the trial and has been requested by this
Consulate to inform the Department in the premises.
7.

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
7. The Sentences:
Keiji Kitada ....... Three months' penal servitude
and fine of ¥952,380 (in case
of failure to pay the fine,
the culprit shall be placed
in a prison workshop for 365
days).
Kanichi Shimamura .. One month's penal servitude
and fine of ¥841,380.
(To be placed under probation
for two years. In case of
failure to pay the fine, the
culprit shall be placed in a
prison workshop for 160 days.)
Gihei Miyamoto ..... Fine of 76,590.
(In case of failure to pay
the fine, the culprit shall
be placed in a prison workshop
for 120 days.)
Shigeichi Ihara .... Fine of ¥8l0,300.
(In case of failure to pay
the fine, the culprit shall
be placed in a prison work-
shop for 150 days.)
Mantaro Asada ...... Fine of 400.
(In case of failure to pay
the fine, the culprit shall
be placed in a prison work-
shop for 80 days.)
Minezo Yajima ...... Fine of ¥500.
(In case of failure to pay
the fine, the culprit shall
be placed in a prison work-
shop for 100 days.)
The magnitude of the above fines is on paper only.
The chief culprit, Kitada, for example, is given the
option of paying the prodigious sum of 952,380 or
spending a year in a penal workshop. Capitalizing his
services at the rate of 2 per day, it is perceived
that with an outlay of 730 he can liquidate an obliga-
tion of 952,380, netting a clear saving of 951,650.
Such arithmetic of course assumes farcical proportions.
The fines are punishment solely in name.
It

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
It does, however, appear that in addition to the
sentences enumerated above, there were imposed by the
court additional fines on the following culprits:
Keiji Kitada
......... 1,182,161.472
Kanichi Shimamura
....
1,423,793.118
Gihei Miyamoto
140,070.00
Shigeichi Ihara
1,366,953.168
These fines are understood to be real. They are derived
from application of the Japanese narcotics laws, whereby
provision is made that all the drugs smuggled by a
convicted narcotics runner must be confiscated or, if
that is. impossible, be compensated for at the market
price for the narcotics in question. Since the gang
had been successful in disposing of most of its
importations from Hamburg, these additional fines
represent the cost of the drugs disposed of. If the
oonvicted men can not satisfy the fines in cash the
State attaches their property in the same manner as if
they had been adjudged bankrupt.
The foregoing paragraphs present the gist of the
court record under the headings which were of particular
interest to the Department. Should this Consulate be
able to secure additional information with reference
to the instant case or to others in connection with
the illegal entry of narcotics, it will at once inform
the Department.
Respegtfully yours,
Honord Ponron
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

KXTRACT FROM AMNUAL REPORT OF THE JAPANESE

In Ponape Island, a custom has been prevalent from
early times to drink the juioe extracted from & perennial
herb ealled Shakao or Sakao by pounding 1 ts roots. This
drink is taken only on such oocasion as marriages, funerals,
the sonstruetion of new houses, the first use of fishing nets,
the repairing of roads and the olearing of undergrowth in
palm forests, when people assemble and take meals together,
but it is seldom indulged in at private homes.
When an occasion oomes on whioh the drink is needed, they
first gather roots of the herb and after cutting them into
suitable sizes, pound them on a flat stone with a stone as
large as a cooa-nut (when 1t is to be used in conneotion with
a oeremonial, those pounding the roots as well as those stand-
ing beside them, all wearing waist-cloth made of palm leaves,
sing certain fixed songs in chorus). After the roots are
erashed to a suitable degree, a little water is added to them,
and the pulp being wrapped up in leaves of a tree called
Karao (or those of ohohamabou) the juice is wrung out by hand
into shells of cocoa-nuts. It is then taken in order of the
mocial rank of the drinkers. Tomen also drink it, but in a
far leas quantity than men.
shakao is a plant belonging to the species of pepper and
is seientirieally called piper methysthicum forst. It is
known among natives of the Pacific islands by suoh names as
kava, ave, awa or kawakawa. It grows wild, but in the Island
of Ponape natives grow it in palm forests or ordinary farms
by planting outtings.
It is one to three metres in height. Several stalks
with the maximum diameter of three centimetres come out from
one stump. The leaves are of irregular cordate shape. The
roots are two to three oentimetres thick and are of irregu-
lar shape. Their juice has a certain smell and a sharp bit-
tor taste with a refreshing fluvour. It is called by natives
kave or kawakawa wine and is widely used by natives of the
Polymesian islands. It causes a slight excitement to the
drinker and gradually makes him sleepy. Among its ingredient
is a resin called methyaticin, whioh is of two kinds of L and
B. L narootizes the tongue and eye. According to the studies
so far made, the kawa acid (C sH O), which orystallizes
whem it is dissolved, causes f8caf Baralysis. There is also
an opimion that it is a vertain sugary ingredient that is re-
spomsible for the action.

FINE ON SMUGGLERS
IS UPHELD BY COURT
Six Men Accused of Evading
Narcotics Rules Ordered to
Pay ¥6,802,367
The Supreme Court yesterday after-
noon turned down the appeal of six
Japanese drug smugglers, thereby up-
holding sentence of the District Court
for fines totaling ¥2,681,460 and recom-
pense to the State to be paid by the
defendants amounting to ¥4,119,907.
The men were accused of violation of
the narcotics law and also customs re-
gulations.

gled into Japan from Germany during
the three years between February, 1928
and February, 1931, 3,784 kilograms of
morphine compounds in 67 consign-
ments. The defendants are:
Kyoji Kitada, 55. real estate dealer.
of Okumicho, Osaka. Sentenced to
three months imprisonment, to pay
a fine of ¥952,380 and for recompense
to the State an additional ¥1,182,161.
Mantaro Asada, 35, a druggist, of
Awaza. Osaka. Sentenced to pay a fine
of ¥400.

Minami-cho. Aoyama, Akasaka, Tokyo.
Sentenced to one month's imprison-

793.

2AIRS
JAN 3 1934
Supertmene at Stare
New Example of Judgment on Smuggling
of Morphine.
CHUGAI SHOGYO,
234
December 11, 1933.
There was a judgment case on smuggling of opium
BH
at the Supreme Court which was in charge of S. Motoji,
the presiding judge. This case was previously appealed
but was tejected by theSupreme Court and the court de-
cided to impose a fine of Yen 2,680,000 and also supple-
8
mentary collection of Yen 4,120,000. This sample became
6
an example for future judgments. The defendants are Kyoji
94.114 NARCO
Kitada, 72 Okumi-machi, Nishi-ku, Osaka and six others.
They smuggled Benzol Morphine and Benul (?) Morphine from
Hamburg Germany from 1926 - 1931.
The reason for this judgment, referring to the standard
value of item 1, Article 83 of the Tariff Law, becoming an
example for similar cases that may occur in future, is that
the attorney for the defendants not being statisfied with
the original judgment appealed to the higher court as the
valuation of morphine, according to the attorney's convic-
tion, should be based either on the price of morphine at
1
the time judgment was made or the market price of the prohibi
ed article. However the Supreme Court rejected the appeal and
made the present judgment of the Supreme Court final and an
example for future cases.
894.114 han.
02
The so-called price is the market price at the time
the crime was committed and is not the price at the time of
judgment. This is clear according to the Tariff Law.
However it may be further said that there is no market vaiue,
the prohibited article can be imported if the permit is granted
by the Home Minister and business can be transacted. Therefore,
naturally there is a market value or price. The supplementary
collection must be calculated from the market price at the
time the crime was committed less import duty.

MORPHINE FROM JAPAN
PROBLEM TO AMERICA
Narcotics Commissioner's Re-
port Says Heaviest Shipments
Came From This Country
Nippon Dempo-United Press
WASHINGTON, December 4.—Mor-
phine from Japan constitutes a sub-
stantial part of the supply of dope
smuggled into the United States, the
Federal Narcotics Commissioner, Mr.
Harry J. Anslinger, says in his annual
report, published here today. The
heaviest seizures of illegal morphine
during the past year were effected on
the Pacific coast, and the contraband
"came almost entirely from Japan,
from where it was brought by ship,"
the report says.
"The appearance of Japanese mor-
phine. smuggled, distributed and ped-
dled by Japanese on the Pacific coast
and in Hawaii marks a new develop-
ment in this illicit traffic, which the
American and Canadian authorities
view with grave concern," Mr. Ansl-
C
inger's report adds.

The Japam Aevertiser,

JAN 1 5 34
MORPHINE RUNNER SEIZED
H
Reactionary
Unwittingly
Benefits
Communist Finances
JAN 25 1934
How a member of a reactionary party
894.114 NARCOTiCS/107
unwittingly benefited the funds of an
opposition Communist organization
through a deal in smuggled morphine
was revealed in Tokyo Thursday, ac-
cording to the Asahi, when Toyoji
Harada, reactionary, was placed under
arrest.
Police reports said that Harada last
April called at a chemist's shop in
Yodobashi and offered for sale a packet
which he said contained morphine and
for which he wanted ¥500. He ex-
plained that it had been smuggled in
through Saghalien. He left the stuff
with the chemist and on a return call
asked that it be returned to him.
Then he claimed the chemist had sub-
stituted a mixture of sugar and flour
for the narcotic. The chemist called
the police.
Examination revealed that Harada
had come into possession of four
pounds of smuggled morphine in March

The Reorientation of "Dope"
Since the international narootio oonvention went into
foree last July the manufaoturing countries have submitted
reports to the Central Board at Geneva showing a 17 per
cent deerease in morphine production and a 14 per oent decrease
in cocaine output. The supply has fallen off to e point, es
our local police know, where the "dope" -- mostly heroin dope --
whioh peddlers supply to addiots at outrageous prices is
10 per cent narootio and 90 per cent adulterant. This is,
of course, prompting a demand among addiots for cures, and
the Federal government is meeting this demand with hospital-
ization as rapidly as possible.
Side by side with this good news, however, the Treasury
Department's latest report on "Traffic in Opium and Other
Dangorous Drugs" points out that Japanese morphine, peddled
by Japanese agents, has for the first time been appearing in
Hawai1 and on the Pacifio Coast in some quantity. Out of
Geneva also come periodical plaints about the quantities of
Japanese morphine and heroin that are easily available in
China, and also about the Manohurian opium and drug monopoly
whieh is beyond the League's surveillance.
These reports would be folly to belittle. If Japan makes
"depe" eheap and easily available in Asia, and if Amerioa's
European mupply is out off, a stiff ourrent of this traffie
aoress the Pacific must set in, under a foroe as elemental as
the foree of gravity. But if we look into the future this
really is not the most serious aspect of the Oriental "dope"
problem. The Japanese share in this traffio Japan can control;
and umder the pressure of sufficient publioity and moral force
she will do so--sooner or later. But if China gets into this
traffie, with her enormous, offioially patronized opium pro-
duetion (whioh 1s eight times that of all the rest of the
world), and with her growing output of "moonshine" morphine
and heroin--also under irresponsible official patronage--what
foroe on earth 1s going to disoipline her?
Chinese agents in Geneva, whenever they propound this
question to themselves, are in panioky haste to raise a bar-
rage of charges against Japan and against the Occidental com-
mumities in China, in anticipation of the attention that must
soon be fooused upon their country. The police of every foreign
concession or settlement in China waste three-fifths of of their
time trying to proteet their Chinese residents against the
overflow of opium and narootics from the surrounding Chinese
cemmumities, within whioh a rake-ofr from the "dope" trafrie
is the riehest of all ofrioial perquisites.
Praetieally the whole body of China's superfluous
soldiery is maintained out of opium taxes in the Western
provimees or out of opium transit oharges in the littoral
provinees. The eap1 tal at Nanking sits on the biggest artery
of the traffie. Every foreign-language journal published in
ch1n

emormous movemonts of opium toward the coast and about the
imereasing native narootic production -- in competition

each carrying eighty-three pounds of opium, under an escort

THE SECRETARY OF STATE,

WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to inform the Department that on
November 29, 1933 the Kawaguchi Police of Osaka, Japan,
arrested a Chinese opium peddler and the Japanese from
whom he procured the drug. It is thought that the
particulars of the case may be worthy of addition to
0
the Department's files on narcotics.
The local press announced on December 2, 1933 that
the drug peddlers had been engaged in the opium traffic
for the past ten years, during which period they had
disposed of an estimated ¥50,o00 of opium to Chinese
residents of Osaka.
The Governor of Osaka Prefecture has informed this
Consulate that the Chinese peddler is one Rin Eki Sui,
alias Rin/Ko Ji, according to the Japanese pronunciation
of the characters of his name. A Canton Chinese has
FEB 15 1934
FILED
informed this office that the pronunciation in his
dialect would be Ling Cheng Sui alias Ling Hing Che. In
both cases it will be observed that the Japanese and
Chinese

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Chinese pronunciation agrees for the surname. The
Chinese characters are as follows:
林亦水
林興治
This Chinese drug peddler is forty-nine years of
age and is a native of Fukien Province. He was arrested
while in possession of 79 momme (296 grammes) of opium.
The drug had been purchased from a Japanese, one Manjiro
Nakao, information concerning whom the Governor's office
evidently prefers not to impart.
The Chinese has been remanded for trial by the
District Procurator under the provisions of Article 36
of the Japanese opium law. As the trial has not yet
taken place, his eventual punishment is yet undetermined.
It is perhaps significant that the Japanese dealer
in opium from whom the drug was purchased and whose
degree of guilt might properly have been deemed greater
than that of the subsidiary vendor, was let off with a
fine of but 250. This sentence, according to the
Prefectural Government, is final and apparently closes
the case so far as Manjiro Nakao is concerned.
Should further developments become manifest in the
case they will promptly be reported to the Department.
Respectfully yours,
Howard Donovan,

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
THE PEIPING CHRONICLE, SATURDAY,
MARCH 24, 1934.
Japanese in the Dope-Drug Trade.
Speoial to the Peiping Chronicle
Cases in Point
HARBIN, March 17.--Would you not be amazed and, in a sense,
shooked to see, quite by chance, at the police station,
a venerable old man, and seemingly not on the lowest rung
of the sooial ladder, leading a chained young man? And
asking the officer in charge to lock up the young man, who
happens to be his son, for a couple of months, as the last
and sole means of curing the young man's narcotic habits?
Such a scene took place recently at the local police station
when a Russian brought his son, a young man of 20 years old
to the station, chained as if he were a slave with long and
rusty shackles, and asked the officer to lock up the captive.
Since the narcotic vice is not a crime in "Manchukuo"
but, in case of the opium smoking, is on the contrary a
substantial item of fiscal revenue, the police could not oblige
the old man by jailing his son, and the couple were sent back
to find other means of salvation. A moment after, the addiot
himself appeared before the officer and asked him to jail him
for a month or two as the sole means of curing him of the
habit. In another case, the distressed father hired a special
attendant, of athletic proportions, to watch every movement
of his son; this, however, was utterly futile for the young
man, at the first unguarded moment, ran away and was next
day found in a well known dungeon in the last stage of stupor.
Harbin a Distributing Centre
This city has long been noted as a distributing centre of
a thriving narootic trade. Despite the real efforts of the
former Chinese authorities, it has never been suppressed, the
prime reason for this being the fact that the trade has large-
ly been in the Japanese hands. Now, with the legalization of
the opium smoking, when one may buy from the government agents
as much opium as one's pocket will permit, the evil is spread-
ing with disastrous rapidity, affecting the hitherto immune
class of population--the Russians. The possibility of pro-
ouring opium at every turn has led to the opening of innumerable
"seoret" drug factories, turning out opium derivatives in
quantities surficient to poison the entire population of Man-
churia; and the owners and operators of these factories again
happen to be the Japanese subjeots.
One of these hideous places, through which the drug is
being infused into the entire Harbin population, and which
is likened by many to a gigantio spider whose web is thrown
over the entire town, is situated in the centre of the city--
in the alleyway opening into Novogorodnaya street and is
owned and operated by a Japanese. It was opened not long
ago by a newoomer and is registered as a pharmacy. At least
so says a huge sign hanging over the door. If you stand near
by and wateh the shop, you will be amazed to see what a roar-
ing trade it does. It sells everything appertaining to the
drug traffio--pipes, syringes, drugs of all sorts and des-
oriptions, and in quantities as large as one's purse can
stand, retail and wholesale. Here is a good ground for the
Opium

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Opium Commission of the League of Nations to come and in-
vestigate! I do not wish to say anything about the birth
control appliances, all "made in Japan", which constitute
no small branoh of the business of this worthy, and would
make a good acquisition for any museum dealing with sex
perversions.
Besides being a shop, the pharmacy maintains, in the
rear a sort of divan, or a room, wherein one may indulge in
his or her drug--taking habits free of any interference,
quite openly, and as long as one's health will stand it.
Everybody is welcome--there is no distinotion of age, sex,
or social position. Bring only money; if you have none,
being with you goods or anything valuable, and it will be
"swapped" here, on the spot, for drugs.
Verified Report of Typical "Pharmaoy"
To verify the correotness of the report, the writer has
made a little exploration, and is amazed to see that such
a horrid thing is being openly allowed in a country aspiring
to a place among the civilized nations of the world. From
outside, the shop does not attract attention; it is like a
hundred and one similar shops to be found in every Japanese
town. But the hideousness begins the moment the visitor goes
in and exohanges with the proprietor, or with the attendant,
a sort of cabalistic sign. Then the visitor is led into the
back room, rather dark, and filled by some twenty or thirty
persons, of all ages and nationalities, sitting, lying, or
walking in an atmosphere of dense and suffocating oloud.
Now and then he hears a suppressed inarticulate murmur of the
victim just coming out to the grim realities of life.
I have said that, if the visitor has no money, he may
bring anything valuable and "swap" it for the drug. In fact,
there is a Chinese attendant, a hardened criminal to all ap-
pearance, who "appraises" the artioles brought and haggles
with the viotims. Shaking hands offer to him watohes, rings,
spoons, articles of apparel, often stolen, whioh the next
day find their way into the pawn-shops.
Similar shops can be found in every street of the city,
and, in ninety-five cases out of one hundred, they are run
by Japanese or Koreans. Some of them bear tobacconists',
photo-dealers', stationers' or other signs, and a surprising
side of it lies in the fact that their real trade is well
known to the police, who do nothing to suppress it--it simply
does not concern them.

THE PEIPING CHRONICLE, Tuesday, Maroh 37, 1934.
16 Arrests Made In Drug Factory Raid in
Peiping
1,Oo0 Ounces of Heroin Seized by Police inside Ho Ping Men
Den Registered as Tooth Powder Establishment by Rich
Proprietor
Central News
PEIPING, March 25. - Good work on the part of plain-
clothes deteotives paved the way to the disoovery and raid
last night of a big heroin dealers' den at house No. 18b,
Hou Hsi Wa Chang, inside the Ho Ping Men (Gate of Peace).
More than 60 packages of heroin (about 1,000 ounoes)
valued at over $30,000, were seized and Liu Jung-hsiang,
the proprietor, together with fifteen workers, was taken
to the police station. Liu, 30, a native of Honan, is
stated to have amassed quite a fortune in this illicit trade.
The place was registered as a tooth powder shop, Liu
outwardly doing business as a dealer in tooth powder of
the "Incomparable" brand. Because he had a very systematic
way of carrying on the trade and escaping the notice of the
polioe, the den was not discovered until last night.
Drug Hidden in Tooth Powder
Liu, it is learned, had been purohasing tooth powder
in large quantities, and then putting heroin in it. The
packages bore the "Ínoomparable" mark. Selling them in
this way had not aroused the attention of the keepers of law.
It was only recently that visits to the place of cus-
tomers of various descriptions attracted the notice of the
police, who then kept a close watoh over the movements of
Liu who went about town in motor-oar.
Yesterday the polioe learned that Liu had reoeived a
new shipment of tooth powder from Tientsin. The goods
arrived at 4 o'olook and it was noticed during their un-
loading that the coolies looked suspicious. At 8 o'clook
a polioe unit broke into Liu's house and disoovered the
iliegal drug.
Prisoners Handed Over to Court
Router
PEIPING, Maroh 26. - Fifteen persons are held in the
Bureau of Public Safety on charges of manufacturing and
traffioking in heroin, known to the Chinese as "white powder."
The

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.96)
-2
The prisoners were arrested on Saturday night when
a party of deteotives raided a house in the south city
and seized more than 1,000 ounces of heroin valued at
#305.000.
The proprietor of the factory, who is among the
arrested, posed as a dealer in tooth powder. After a
preliminary examination the prisoners will be handed
over to the Peiping District Court for trial and punish-
mont.

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
PEKING AND TIENTSIN TIMES, WEDNESDAY, MARCH 28, 1934.
ANTI-DRUG CAMPAIGN.
Initiated by Peiping Authorities.
"Dope-Cure" Hospitals Urged.
Clean-Up Causes Panic Among Officials.
Peiping, Mar. 36.
A drastic anti-drug campaign has been initiated by the
authorities of the various official organs in the old capi-
tal. It is understood that the chiefs of various depart-
ments have recently received a strict order from the National
Government holding them responsible for the suppression of
the drug evil among the subordinates under them within a
certain time limit.
At the same time another circular order has been re-
ceived from Nanking by the Municipal Government instructing
it to devise means to establish anti-drug hospitals for the
ouring of the opium habit within two months.
The authorities have been warned that in spite of the
repeated admonitions of the Central Government, the drug
evil has been steadily increasing in an alarmingly rapid
speed until now a large section of members in the civil
service are adddbted to one or the other drug, at the
connivance of their superiors.
The renascence of the nation will be seriously im-
peded if this deplorable condition of affairs be allowed
to continue unchecked.
After the issuance of this order, the authorities of
the various organs shall make a thorough investigation of
the number of members in their employment who are addicted
to the opium habit and force them to get rid of the evil
within a time limit, failing which summary dismassal will
be inflicted.
Subsequently a general anti-opium campaign is now being
started by all official departments. Batohes of subordinate
officials who are known to be drug habitues have been sent
to hospitals for treatment, while each member is required
to afford guarantee of five other colleagues that he is a
non-opium smoker.
A panio is now prevailing among official circles and a
number of opium habitues for fear of being dismissed have
entered hospital voluntarily or are taking medicines pri-
vately to rid themselves of this vice. According to the
regulations promulgated all those who are suspected of be-
ing opium smokers are required to undergo a medical examina-
tion.
It

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
It is also learned that similar drastic action has
also been adopted in many other provinces and municipali-
ties and a large number of officials have been either dis-
missed or censured for opium smoking or drug taking. On
the other hand opium is still sold publicly in many prov-
inces under a semi-government monopoly while poppy is
planted under the encouragement of the local militarists
in the western and northwestern frontier regions and im-
ported into the coastal and interior provinces on the pay-
ment of a tax. At the same time huge quantities of opium
are "smuggled" into Chine by the Japanese and Koreans
every year while heroin and its derivative "Chintan" which
are much more destructive than the evil of opium after
establishing a firm footing in North China are infesting
the regions along the two banks of the Yangtze River
rapidly.
In view of the great convenience and the easiness to
escape detection, the majority of the opium smokers have
taken to heroin since the last few years. Heroin manufac-
tories are known to have been established within the walls
of city and every large town is doing a very prosperous
business, but the local authorities seem to be powerless
in dealing with them since the majority of them are run
by Japanese or Koreans. The utmost the polic can do is
to arrest those Chinese who deal with such drug producers
on their departure from the doors of the manufactories.
- Asiatio.

SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
instruction of September 25, 1933 (File No. 894.114-
24
894.114
Narcotics), requesting full details of the trial and
conviction in Osaka of a gang of narcotics smugglers
on August 15, 1933, and to this Consulate's despatches
101
Nos. 240 of October 24, 1933, and 262 of December 11,
1933, in reply to that instruction.
Another member of the same group of smugglers and
evidently the co-leader of the gang was convicted by
the Osaka District Court on July 17, 1934 to serve a
sentence of three months' penal servitude and to "pay"
a fine of ¥952,380 and a forfeit of ¥1,586,193.11.
This man was Sennosuke Asai, aged 39, the co-proprietor
of a large dance hall at Amagasaki, near Osaka and the
collaborator of Keiji Kitada who was the chief culprit
SEF1 4 934
in the case reported upon in the despatches cited above.
FILED
Asai, who had been hunted by the Police since
February

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
February, 193l, voluntarily surrendered himself to the
authorities on April 4, 1934 and was indicted in Osaka
for smuggling 4,292 kilogrammes of benzyl morphine
from Hamburg in conjunction with the previously
convicted Keiji Kitada and his confederates. The
transcript of the court record and sentence, furnished
the Consulate by the Chief Procurator of the Osaka
District Court, reveals that the sources of supply were
the same as indicated in Despatch No. 240 under reference:
Leopold Mager, Hamburg.
E. Paul, Hamburg.
Rudowitchgrafe or Ludowici Grefe
(translation from Japanese
dubious), Hamburg.
According to Chief Prosecutor Ryohei Wada, the drugs
smuggled by Asai and Kitada were disposed of as follows:
"With reference to the disposition of the
narcotics smuggled, you are informed that
Benzyl Morphine (390 kilogrammes in quantity)
has been seized as evidence, Benzol Morphine
(about 450 kilogrammes in quantity) was
disposed of at Dairen, and the rest of the
smuggled narcotics, namely, about 2,387
kilogrammes of Benzyl Morphine and about
1,065 kilogrammes of Benzyl Morphine, was
sold through brokers to Sajiro Doi Shoten,
Koraibashi-dori 2-chome, Higashi-ku, Osaka,
and other drug stores, and resold to
pharmaceutical companies. It is believed
that the narcotics sold to the pharmaceutical
companies were refined into heroin and resold
to the drug stores for public use."
The benzyl morphine seized as evidence was ordered
confiscated by the District Court.
The sentence imposed upon Asai was almost identical
with that given Kitada a year ago. The term of penal
servitude -- three months -- and the fine -- ¥952,380
were identical. Asai was assessed a slightly higher
forfeit of 1,586,193.1l, as compared with Kitada's
forfeit

=== Page 250 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
forfeit of 1,182,161.47. It is understood that this
forfeit is computed from the market value of the drugs
successfully disposed of by Asai.
As in the case of Kitada the sentence, so imposing
when reported in the newspapers, is largely sham. Thus,
the court ruled that Asai might apply the forty days he
had already spent in jail awaiting trial to the sentence
of three months penal servitude. As in the instance of
his friend Kitada the "fine" of ¥952,380 may be
liquidated by serving 365 days in prison. Messrs. Asai
and Kitada are therefore earning 2,609 a day by
remaining in jail. Mr. Asai, it may be pointed out,
has not yet commenced to liquidate his fine, as he was
released on bail within two days after the imposition
of sentence, preparatory to making appeal to the Osaka
Court of Appeals. In regard to this appeal it should be
pointed out (cf. Despatch No. 262) that in the identical
case the appeal of Kitada et al was denied by the
Japanese Supreme Court; an indication of the probable
fate of Asai's action.
With reference to the payment of the forfeit of
1,586,193.11 it is understood that if the person
convicted is unable to pay his property is subject to
attachment by the State in satisfaction of the entire
sum.
Further developments in regard to the appeal of
Asai will be reported in due course.

=== Page 256 === (confidence 1.00)
(Translation)
PROSPECTUS OF THE ORGANIZATION OF AN
ASSOCIATION FOR THE RELIEF OF NARCOTIC
ADDICTS.
Although the general public is afraid of infectious
diseases, the (Japanese) people seem to be quite indifferent
to the effects of narcotics which contain opium in large
percentages. Nothing can be more absurd than this attitude.
Not only in Chosen, but in Japan Proper as well, narcotics
addicts are increasing in number gradually. An investiga-
tion made by the Osaka Municipality in 1929 indicated that,
in that year, there were some 3,700 narcotics addicts in
Osaka. In 1932 the authorities in Tokyo also conducted an
investigation, when it was discovered that in Tokyo and the
immediate neighborhood some 3,000 out of 34,400 Korean
laborers were addicted to the use of narcotics. It is not
difficult to surmise that such addicts can also be found
among laboring and other classes in Yokohama, Kobe, Fuku-
oka and other large cities of Japan. As time goes on the
number of addicts is rapidly increasing.
It is hardly necessary to point out that persons ad-
dicted to the use of narcotics either become fatally poisoned
or become incapacitated for work of any kind. There have
been many instances of poverty-stricken sufferers from nar-
cotics poisoning resorting to theft and other offenses in an
effort to obtain money with which to purchase drugs or medi-
cines, in order to relieve their sufferings. Even when
arrested and detained by the police authorities they exhibit
peculiar symptoms, because of the lack of their habitual
drugs, and have behaved as if insane, eventually collapsing
entirely. The police have therefore been obliged to free
them. Once released, they have repeated their offenses
against the law.
The question naturally arises as to whether or not
there are laws and regulations providing for the relief and
protection of sufferers from narcotics poisoning in Japan.
No such regulations are yet in force. There are only two
or three private relief establishments which have been or-
ganized by Japanese and Korean individuals in recent years
and which are imperfect in their functioning. As a natioanl
organization, the present Association was formed in Tokyo
in 1933 for the treatment of sufferers from narcotics poison-
ing and for the suppression of narcotics addiction.
Regulations of the Association for the
Relief of Narcotics Addicts.
Art. 1. The Association shall be named "The Associa-
tion for the Relief of Narcotics Addicts" (Mayaku Chudoku-
sha Kyugo-Kai).
Art. 2.

=== Page 257 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
Art. 2. The Association shall have its offices in the
Tokyo Young Men's Christian Association Building at No. 3,
Sanchome, Mitoshiro-cho, Kanda, Tokyo.
Art. 3. The Association has for its object the pro-
tection and treatment of addicts and sufferers from the
habitual use of morphine, heroin, cocaine and other nar-
cotics containing a large percentage of opium, as well as
the suppression of the habitual use of narcotic drugs.
Art. 4. The Association shall fulfill the following
functions in the attainment of its objects:
a. The establishment of offices for the treatment
and protection of sufferers from narcotics
addiction.
b. The investigation and study of narcotic drugs
and narcotic poisoning.
c. Social movements for the suppression of the
habitual use of narcotic drugs.
d. The guidance of addicts under treatment.
e. Other matters especially necessary for the
attainment of the above-mentioned objects.
Art. 5. The Association shall have the following
officers:
Directors, less than ten in number.
One Managing Director and two fixed directors.
Several counselors.
Two auditors.
Several advisers.
Art. 6. The term of office of the directors shall be
two years, and one-half shall be elected each year. The
term of office of the counselors shall be three years and
they may stand for re-election.
Art. 7. After the termination of the term of office of
the directors, they shall remain in office until their suc-
cessors have assumed their duties.
Art. 8. The Managing Director shall be the legal re-
presentative of the Association and shall supervise its
affairs. The fixed directors shall manage all affairs of the
Association in accordance with instructions from the Managing
Director. The advisers and counselors shall reply to in-
quiries from the Managing Director in regard to important
affairs of the Association. The auditors shall inspect the
assets andbusiness condtion of the Association.
Art. 9. The directors shall organize a directors'
council and formulate resolutions for the carrying out of
the business of the Association. Meetings of the directors'
council

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
council shall be called by the Managing Director, who
shall assume the dtity of chairman.
Art. 10. The Directors' Council shall meet once each
year, but the Managing Director may, if deemed necessary,
call an extraordinary meeting.
Art. 1l. The members of the Association shall be of
the following three classes:
Honorary members.
Special members.
Ordinary members.
Art. 12. The assets of the Association shall be
composed of the following:
Subscriptions by members.
Subventions from governmental and public
offices as well as contributions from indi-
viduals.
Income from property of various kinds.
Other income.
Art. 13. The expenses of the Association must be
covered by the income.
Art. 14. The assets of the Association (except
immovable property) shall be deposited with banks of good
standing or with post-office savings banks. The assets
may also be converted into international securities or
other negotiable securities of sound character.
Art. 15. The annual budget of the Association shall be
passed by the Directors' Council.
Art. 16. The fiscal year of the Association shall be
from April 1 to March 31 of the following year.
Art. 17. The Association may, if deemed necessary,
appoint a manager or managers, chief clerks, ordinary clerks,
etc.,
Art. 18. Detailed rules for the carrying out of the
rules and regulations relating to accounts shall be especially
drawn up.
Art. 19. The following directors were appointed at the
time of organization of the Association:
Managing Director: Mr. Hanpei Nagao.
Fixed Director: Mr. Takayuki Ikuye.
Directors: Mr. Masasuke Masutomi and seven others.
Auditors:
Mr. Daikichiro Tagawa
Mr. Tsunejiro Matsuyama.
Counselors: 37 names, including Mr. Kenkichi
Yoshizaya,

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Yoshizawa, formerly Minister for Foreign Affairs;
Dr. Bowles; Mrs. Hideko Inouye; Mrs. Yoneko Imai;
Mr. Tsurukichi Maruyama, formerly Superintendent
of the Metropolitan Police; Mr. Choichi Ariyoshi,
formerly Mayor of Yokohama and now a member of the
House of Peers; and Mr. Teizaburo Sekiya, Vice
Minister of the Imperial Household.
REPORT ON RELIEF OPERATIONS.
The Association, which was established on February 17,
1933, opened the first relief station at No. 78 Umeda-cho,
Honda, Kazushika-ku, Tokyo. This station was placed in
charge of a Korean, Cho Sei Ki.
In 1932 there were in Tokyo some 3,000 sufferers from
the effects of the habitual use of narcotics, 90 per cent of
whom were Korean laborers. No accurate statistics are yet
available in regard to the number of such sufferers in all
Japan. Sufferers from narcotics poisoning require about
two yen each day in order to obtain the drugs with which to
free themselves from pain. To satisfy their desire, they
resort to theft and other offences.
In 1933, the Association received from the Department
of Home Affairs a subvention of Yen 1,O00. The Association
also received from the Tokyo Municipality the sum of Yen
1,000 to assist it in carrying out its operations. The
first relief station is composed of rooms for medical treat-
ment, rooms for the medical staff, baths, kitchen, etc
Attention is called to the fact that the staff of the
relief station devised a special medical treatment which
was practiced on sufferers who voluntarily applied for this
special treatment. Under this treatment, sufferers are
confined in special rooms and are prevented from taking nar-
cotics in any form whatever, nurses being constantly in
attendance. After five days, the sufferers exhibit the
peculiar symptoms resulting from the withdrawal of narcotics
and begin to suffer intense pain. After another week of
the special treatment, the sufferers show signs of improve-
ment and are removed to ordinary rooms. The patients are
then compelled to engage in labor for a short time each day,
and after three months more they are usually able to earn
their living as ordinary laborers. Special working quarters
are provided in the relief station, where special work, ac-
cording to the patient's capacity, is assigned to each
patient. Although the methods of treatment are complica-
ted, the medical and other members of the staff of the
relief station are eager to carry out their duties and hope
to enlarge the scope of operations of the station in the
future.
The Association is not yet widely known to the Japanese
public, and therefore the Association is still experiencing
difficulty in collecting contributions from various quar-
ters. It is reassuring, however, to note that both official
and

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
and private quarters, including the Metropolitan Police
Board, are now beginning to support the Association with
the financial aid which it needs.
Following are estimates of the receipts and expen-
ditures of the Association for 1934:

The prospectus continues with the Association's plans
for expansion. It points out that narcotics poisoning is a
serious menace to the Japanese people and that the results
obtained by the relief stations already established were
more successful than anticipated. Its object is to exter-
minate narcotics poisoning and it therefore has a very
great responsibility. It must enlarge the scope of opera-
tions of the relief stations and has mapped out a plan.
It wants to purchase a tract of land on the island of
Oshima; off the Izu Peninsula, and there establish a farm
where loo narcotics addicts can be treated at a time. It
is also hoped to establish another relief station where
70 addicts who are unable to engage in the hard labor of
the farm can be accommodated and given easier forms of
manual labor. In this way, some 200 addicts can be treated
at the same time. As they are expected to recover in four or
five months and be discharged, the scheme will permit some
500 addicts to be treated each year. The Association
solicits contributions to the new project, the cost of which
is estimated to be as follows:
Endowment fund .
Yen 200,000
Cost of property, buildings, etc.
262,780.
Reserves for further expansion..

n
5.000
Total
Yen 477,780.
The prospectus then gives the estimated annual receipts
and expenditures of the expanded scheme.

Kobe, October 1, 1934.
Howard Donovan, Esquire,
American Consul,
Kobe.
Sir:
With reference to your letters of September 27th
and August 2nd and 20th concerning the alloged
smugglers of narcotic drugs, I have the honor to
furnish you with the following information. Please
note that the delay in replying to you was due to
the fact that at the time of receipt of your previous
letters this office was still hunting for some of the
culprits and was not in a position to give information
in the premises.
1. Names, addresses, and occupations of the smugglers:
Shigeo Kawasaki, 38, maker of canvas goods,
1/2 Sakae-machi 3-chome, Hiroshima-shi.
Giiohiro Asahi, 40, druggist,
12 Imazato Kita-dori 3-chome, Higashiyodogawa-ku,
Osaka.
Shoji Takahashi, 48, insurance agent,
7 Chishima-cho l-chome, Minato-ku, Kobe.
Kanjo Yugi, 58, foreign trader,
58 Bingodo, Yawata, Nada-ku, Kobe.
Eizo Matsumoto, 31, fireman on board the
Osaka Shosen Kaisha Steamer BORNEO MARU.
Kosaguma Noguchi, 38, carpenter on board the
Osaka Shosen Kaisha Steamer BORNEO MARU.
Shizuo Okamoto, 37, no occupation, address unknown.
Sadaichi Aiga, 36,
n
n
A certain Morizawa, 65,
n
2. Criminal actions:
1.
Kawasaki, in league with the above-mentioned
Asahi, Okamoto, Morizawa, and members of the crews
of the S.S. BORNEO MARU and other ships, shipped
nareoties without permission of the proper authori-
ties to Seattle, U.S.A., Rangoon, British Dominions,
and Sainan, China, seven times during the period
from

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
from about June, 1929 to about February, 1931.
The narcotics smuggled were:
Hydrochlorate of Coccain -- 36/37 cases
(25 ounces per case)
Hydrochlorate of Heroin --- il casos
(25 ounces per case)
Hydrochlorate of Morphine - 2 cases
(one pound per case)
2.
Takahashi smuggled two cases of hydrochlorate
of coccain (25 ounces per case), l00 bottles of
hydrochlorate of the same drug (five grams per
bottle), and three bottles of hydrochlorate of
morphine (five grams per bottle) to Tacoma, U.S.A.,
and Bombay, British India, in league with Ásahi,
Noguohi, and members of the crew of the O. S. K.
steamer ARABIA MARU, during the period from about
December, 1930 to about January, 1933.
3.
Noguchi obtained three bottles of hydrochlorate
of morphine (5 grams per bottle) from Takahashi
about Ootober, 1932 and smuggled them to Belawan,
Sumatra, carrying them by stealth on board the
BORNEO MARU whereof he was a member of the crew
at that time. Matsumoto obtained one hundred
bottles of hydrochlorate of coccain from Takahashi
about January, 1933 and smuggled them to 'Bombay,
British India, carrying them by stealth on board
the S.S. BORNEO MARU, whereof he was then a member
of the crew.
4.
Aiga, in league with Yugi, smuggled to
Shingishu, Chosen, seven bottles of hydrochlorate
of heroin (5 grams per bottle) which he had
obtained from the above-mentioned Asahi.
In order to make a big fortune, all the culprits
shipped the narcotics without the permission of the
Home Minister, violating the law controlling the
narcotic drugs. After the examinations were made by
the Section of Penal Affairs, of this government, the
case was referred to the Kobe Local Court and it is
expected that these alleged smugglers will shortly
be indicted and brought to trial.

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE
Sept. 21, 1934.
CCT 23 1934
Department of State
As usual in North China opium
scandals, there is a good deal of mutual
recrimination between Japanese and
Chinese regarding the reports of whole-
sale smuggling of Jehol opium over the
Wall. The army authorities are reported
to be very much annoyed at the reports
that opium goes through as "military
stores." It is, however, a very wide-
spread belief in China that the refusal
of the army and navy to allow their
stores to be inspected by the Customs
covers a great deal of smuggling of
dutiable goods. In Shanghai the belief
takes such extravagant forms as as-
severations that the stocks of Japanese
department stores come through in this
privileged manner. It is not to be sup-
posed that the army would condescend
to conspire with commercial people; but
if it claims special privileges fairytales
are sure to be invented.

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE
September 22, 1934.
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Division
OCT 23 1934
DRUG DEALERS
Department of State
MASSACRED
CHINESE FARMERS WIPE OUT A
KOREAN GANG
UNREST IN HOPEI
Rengo to Chronicle.]
Tientsin, Sept. 15.
A Chinese Government official was
interviewed to-day regarding the alleged
murder a few days ago of a Japanese
army contrac.or at Lintoutin, Hopei pro-
vince, and the massacre last week of a
number of Koreans by farmers at
Fushanssu, a town in the Lautung district
of Hopei.
He declared that the Koreans involved
in the Fushanssu incident were heroin
dealers who attempted to intimidate the
villagers because a suspicious militia-man
had fired a warning shot.
The headman of the village, he said,
agreed to pay a certain amoun: of money
to the Koreans in order to be left un-
molested. The Koreans together with
their Chinese comrades, however, de-
manded a larger sum, and the headman
unable to meet their demands, fled from
the village.
He further declared that the Koreans
then seized the villagers, who were later
relcased by the militia. It was then that
the villagers taking the law into their
own hands, allegdly massacred the in-
vaders.
Regarding the death of the army con-
tractor, the official asserted that he had
been murdered by bandits after having
been warned by the militia of the pos-
sibility of danger.
Meanwhile, a conference of representa-
tives of Koreans residing in the de-
militarised zone of Peiping and Tientsn,
was held in the Japanese area of Tientsin
yesterday. It was decided to organise a
committee to watch developments and to
formulate demands, including the re-
covery of the bodies and the punishmen
of the villagers. It was also decided to
demand official guarantees of protection
and compensation for the relatives of the
victims.

AEROPLANES USED FOR DRUG
SMUGGLING
Rengo to Chronicle.1
Canton, Sept. 20.
Aeroplanes are being used for illicit.
drug traffic in Hunan. Kwangtung and
Kwangsi provinces, according to a sensa-
tional story published in to-day's vernacu-
lar newspapers.
It is alleged that large quantities of
opium and heroin from Kweichow are
being transported to Hungkiang, in
eastern Hunan, and from there are dis-
tributed in Kwantung and Kwansi by
aeroplanes.
The story alleges that the recent arrest
of General Huang Fei, chief of the Hunan
Air Corps, is connected with the scandal.
