=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1011 of October 16, 1934,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
TRANSLATION OF NEWS ITEM IN THE OSAKA ASAHI
October 2, 1934.
The MANILA MARU, an emigrant steamship of the Osaka
Shosen Kaisha, arrived at Moji on October lst from Singa-
pore, homeward bound from Brazil. Immediately upon her
arrival, Mr. Hayashi, the Chief Detective of the Kobe
Water Police, aided by two other members of the Kobe Water
Police Station, prohibited the landing of the crew of the
MANILA MARU and conducted a strict search of the steward's
department. They continued the examination on board the
MANILA MARU after she left Moji for Kobe that afternoon.
The step taken by the Kobe Water Police was prompted
by the fact that when the MANILA MARU was en route to
Brazil, on June 18th, in the Indian Ocean, the third-class
stewards in a body attacked and injured the Chief Steward.
Nine of the ringleaders were sent back to Kobe from Mom-
bassa. During the examination of these ringleaders by
the Kobe Water Police, it appears that it was discovered
that the crew of the vessel smuggled opium on a large
scale at Singapore and other places.
Mr. Yanagisawa, the Chief Mate, said that it was true
than on their outward voyage, Kawashige Okayama, a third-
class steward, aged 31, was arrested at Singapore and
sentenced to i8 months' imprisonment for the smuggling of
opium. He did not think, however, that others were in-
volved in the crime. The cause of the attack on the Chief
Steward was some trivial matter, and Mr. Yanagisawa did
not think that it was due to a fight over the distribution
of profits. At any rate, the entire crew is prohibited
from landing and is being examined.

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
COPY
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 1011 of October 11, 1934,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Imerican Consulate, Kobe, Japan,
October 11, 1934.
Edwin L. Neville, Esquire,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim,
American Embassy,
Tokyo.
Sir:
Reference is made to the Embassy's letter of
October 9, 1934, requesting the Gonsulate to call
upon the Kobe Water Police and ascertain the results
of their investigation of the crew of the Osaka
Shosen Kaisha steamer MANILA MARU in connection with
the drug smuggling activities of one Kawashige
Okayama (sic) and his possible confederates among
the crew.
▲ representative of the Consulate has conferred
with the Chief of Detectives of the Kobe Water Police,
Mr. S. Hayashi. This gentleman states that the case
involves solely Kawashige and no other members of the
crew of the MANILA MARU. According to Mr. Hayashi,
Kawashige, whose correct name is Sekiyama Kawashige,
acted as the carrier of a consignment of opium from
Hongkong to Singapore. Evidently the Singapore
police had previous warning from Hongkong, as they
were on hand at Singapore the moment Kawashige
stepped ashore with his opium, a'nd arrested both
him and the person who came to the dock for the
smuggled drugs. Kawashige is still in jail in
Singapore. Mr. Hayashi added that since the offense
was committed abroad and punishment had been inflicted,
the Japanese police would not arrest Kawashige upon
his return to this country. The representative of
the Consulate thought it best not to observe that
part of the illegal act took place upon a Japanese
ship on the high seas and within the jurisdiction of
Japan.
The Chief of Detectives stated that careful
investigation of the attack of the third class cabin
boys on the Chief Steward of the MANILA MARU on June
18th had revealed that the cause was mutual ill
feeling between the boys and the Chief Steward, in
no way connected with the opium smuggling activities
of Kawashige. Mr. Hayashi declared that the cabin
boys would be brought to trial on charges of assault
but that their arrest had nothing to do with drug
smuggling.
Respectfully yours,
(s) Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

November 9, 1934.
PERSONAL AND
In re: Hajime Hoshi - narcotic suspect.
CONFIDENTIAL.
Mr. H. J. Anslinger,
Commissioner of Narcotics,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Anslinger:
Reference is made to your letter of October 12, l934,
relative to suspect Hajime Hoshi. You are advised that this
subject arrived on the TATSUTA MARU on the afternoon of
November 7th and with the assistance of the Customs officers
Narcotic Agents A. W. Roberts and V. D. Carli kept Hoshi under
surveillance on the two days he stopped over in San Francisco.
Hoshi registered at the Sir Francis Drake Hotel where he
interviewed several Japanese, whose names were learned by the
investigating officers in checking automobile license mumbers,
namely: George Sasaki, 472 Sheridan Street, Palo Alto, Calif;
H. N. Nakashita, 6l6 North Sixth Street, San Jose, Calif; Kenji
Kassai, 1l96 Stanyan Street, San Francisco, Calif. These
individuals will be investigated immediately to determine whether
or not they are connected in any way in the handling of narcotic
drugs.
Hoshi left San Francisco on the Lark - Southern Pacific
train - at 8:30 P.M., November 8th for Los Angeles. The Narcotic
Office at Los Angeles was furnished with the necessary information
so that this subject could be investigated during his short stop-
over in Los Angeles. They were also advised to wire you immediately
upon the departure of Hoshi for the east, giving you baggage
mmbers, if any, and the pullman space oscupied by him. Before
leaving San Francisco Hoshi checked a large suit case on his ticket
with the Union Transfer Company of this city, the check number
being 906436. The suit case is a large one and is checked to New
York but it may be taken off at Los Angeles and re-checked, if de-
sired.
On this date I was advised by Frederick S. Freed, Supervising
Customs Agent, San Francisco, that the Customs officers searched the
baggage of Hoshi and in one of his hand bags certain pharmaceutical
preparations were examined and tested but failed to show any narcotic
drugs. However, the Customs officers found among the pharmaceutical

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
preparations one bottle containing 1/4 ounce of cocaine for
which he had no permit and for that reason was seized by the
Customs officers.
Hoshi holds what is known as a transpacific ticket with
stop-over privileges to New York. He has been routed over the
Southern Pacifio to Los Angeles, Santa Fe to Chicago, and Big
Four to New York.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) D. A. Bagley,
Acting District Supervisor
District No. 15.

ce Sup. Cus. Agent.

Drug Addicts Inoreasing
LOCAL AUTHONITIES UHGD TO TAIL. GTRICT ACTION
Due to the alurming inorease in the number of opium
and other narcovic poisoning cases among the middle-
olass, the lumpen ano Korean labourers, the liyogo pre-
feotural authorities have been ilstructed by the Home
Orfioe to enforce strict regulations fron next year, in
conneotion with the sale of these drugs by drugstores
and drug manufaoturers, as well as the treatment by
dootors of such patients.

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.97)
COPY:EMP
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE
December 2, 1934.
There is said to be an amount of drug addiotion
in Japan suffioiently serious to attract the attention
and exoite the alarm of the authorities. Members of
the middle ulass and Korean labourors are said to pro-
vide most example. or many years the Japanese authori-
ties were content to allow people to make profits by
indulging in a trade that, while it might be legal up to
a oertain point, left legality behind when it came to
the disposal of the drug. The nstural result of this
is that the people who handled it experimen ted with it.
There is no inherent moral quality that prevents Japanese
from beooming addicts. Opportunity is all that is needed,
and we are seeing today the resul ts of past years when
little trouble was taken because there was no visible
addiotion.

OPIUM SMUGGLERS SEIZED
Japanese and Chinese Are Accused
in Big Casc
Thirty Japanese and Chinese are be-
ing held at the Mita police station :
Tokyo on a charge of smuggling a large
amount of opium from Japanese Sagha-
alien and disposing of it among smok-
ers in Yokohama. Kobe and Osaka.
The leader in the organization of the
ring is said to be Yang Chin-sheng. 58.
who began his activities several years
ago. It is reported the gang leased a
plot of ground near the Japanese
Saghalien border, and grew poppies
with Korean labor. The product was
brcught into Japan proper in shoe-
polish cans, and distributed from a
Chinese restaurant at Yamashita-cho,
Yokohama.

FAR FASTERN AFFAIRS
Division of
DEC 28 1934
Department of State
GANG OF OPIUM SMUGGLERS IN CHINATOWN
BOUGHT THE OPIUM FROM KOREANS IN SAGHALIEN
GANG OF MORE THAN THIRTY ARRESTED IN YOKOHAMA.
The Peace Preservation Section of the Mita (Tokyo) Police
Station is examining Yo Kin Sei (Japanese pronunciation of a
Chinese name) of Yamashita-cho, Yokohama, the ringleader of a
gang of opium smugglers with headquarters in Yokohama's China-
town, and six other Chinese men and women, as well as Moriji
Oki (age 32), of 10 Hinode-cho, Naka-ku, Yokohama, the head of
a certain trading company; Masayoshi Tawara (age 23), of Toyo-
hara-machi, Japanese Saghalien; Nakakichi Muroi (age 48), of
no permanent domicile; Mitsuro Arita (age 37), former detective
of the Kanagawa Prefectural Police Bureau; Shiro Ichikawa (age
43), former detective of the Metropolitan Police Board (Tokyo);
and 23 other Japanese. They were all sent to the Procurator's
office with the necessary evidence, on charges of having vio-
lated the laws governing opium.
The principal offender, Yo Kin Sei (Yang Chin-sheng?),
while keeping a chop suey house called "Kakushin-ro" in Yama-
shita-cho, Yokohama, became acquainted with the above-named
Tawara. Meantime Tawara was also organizing a gang and was
shipping orude opium, which he bought of Koreans in an opium
den in Japanese Saghalien, near the boundary line, at 30 sen
per momme. The opium was shipped in empty shoe-blacking tins

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
2.
and candy jars, which were carefully wrapped in oil cloth
to prevent the odor of opium from esoaping. These tins and
jars were shipped to Japan Proper by tramp steamships, prin-
cipally American. (Note: As far as the Embassy is aware, no
American tramp steamships ever run between Japanese Saghalien
and Japan Proper. Moreover, an American vessel would not be
permitted under the law to carry cargo from one Japanese port
to another. The above statement is therefore obviously er-
roneous.) Yo Kin Sei, upon receipt of the crude opium, had it
refined by his gang in the attic of his house and, mixing the
opium with varnish, flour and red clay, had it made up into a
sort of cake. These cakes were sold to Chinese and Japanese
men of leisure in Kobe, Yokohama and Shibaura (Tokyo) at a
price of Yen 3.00 per momme. The total quantity sold in this
way aggregates 150 kwan (about 1240 1bs.). Yo Kin Sei in this
manner easily made a profit of Yen 100,000.
The Mita police first arrested Shoichi Asano (age 35) of
the gang on October 27, 1934, at Shibaura, while he was selling
opium to a certain Chinese, and since then have made more than
ten trips to Yokohama's Chinatown to arrest the members of the
gang. During that period Tawara was in Japanese Saghalien and
was arrested by the Otomari police at the request of the Mita
Police Station.

Sir:
Reference is made to this Consulate's letter
of November 2, 1934, reporting the trial and
conviction by the Osaka District Court of
Katsutoshi Tsunemitsu, a smuggler of narcotic
drugs.
There is enclosed a copy of a letter from Mr.
Ryohei Wada, the Chief Public Procurator of the

Tsunemitsu was denied by the Osaka Court of Appeals

thesame set of facts, it would appear probable
that Tsunemitsu's appeal will receive a similar
decision.

SIR:
I have the honor to inform the Department of the
arrest and conviction of certain drug smugglers at Kobe,
Japan for traffic in narootics with Netherland India.
From September 21, 1932 to April 9, 1933 four
5
6
members of the crew of the Nanyo Yusen Kaisha freighter
CHERIBON MARU, which trades regularly from Kobe to the
Netherland Indies, were engaged in the smuggling of
morphine from Kobe to Surabaya and Batavia. The
894.114har
nareoties disposed of amounted to a total of thirteen
pounds. It was secured from a tobacconist in Kobe by
the name of Kimura and was peddled by the following
members of the crew of the CHERIBON MARU:
Michikado Iwamoto (age 42) Chief Steward.
Kinjiro Sasamoto (age 38)
Bosun.
Kikuchi
Ordinary Seaman.
FILEDM
Masato Shigeta (age 41)
Donkeyman.
2
1935
None of these persons had a previous criminal record.
The

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The gang was placed under arrest by the Kobe Water
Police late in October, 1934. The tobacconist, Kimura,
died during the course of the subsequent investigation.
The donkeyman, Shigeta, evidently escaped, as the
Prefectural Government has informed the Consulate that
he "1s missing". On December 7, 1934 the Kobe District
Court imposed a sentence of one month's imprisonment on
Iwamoto, Sasamoto and Kikuchi. The sentence was sus pended,
however, pending appeal. According to the Hyogo
Prefeotural Government, the maximum sentence the culprits
could have received under the Narcotios Ordinance of the
Home Department would have been three months.
Sufficient reference has been made in previous
despatches from this Consulate to the purely nominal
punishment inflieted by Japanese courts on convicted
smugglers of narcoties. It is interesting to bear in
mind that in the present case the country of origin of
the morphine was Japan.
The Consulate regrets to report that the attitude
of the local authorities has. been most discouraging in
assisting the Consulate to obtain information concerning
the activities of the smugglers of the CHERIBON MARU.
A request made of the Chief of the Water Police on
October 26, 1934 for data concerning the case was
completely ignored. The Governor of the Prefecture was
next approached and no acknowledgment was made of the
receipt of the Consulate's letter. Eventually, in
response to inquiries from this office, the Prefectural
Government stated that the original letter from the
msulate had been "lost". In all, the Consulate
addressed

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
addressed four letters to the Governor of Hyogo Prefecture
before the information set forth above was at last obtained
on February 4, 1935, three months after the Prefectural
Government was apprised of the Consulate's interest in the
case. It seems evident, however, that the officials of
the Hyogo Kencho have now come to realize that this office
has a justifiable interest in narcotics cases, owing to the
international character of the traffic, and that in the
future its representations will meet with more prompt
satisfaction.
Respectfully yours,
Imord Pimron
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

THE CHINA WEEKLY REVIEW
January 12, 1935
"OPIUM DEN" FOUND ON BRITISH BOAT IN OSAKA HARBOR
Suspicion as to the unusually large crew on board the
B.s. CHaksaNG, 2,358 ton freighter of the Indo-Chine
Steam Navigation company, upon its arrival in Osaka
on the morning of December 24 with miscellaneous cargo
from Shangha1, led officers of the foreign seotion of
the Osaka water police, while on an inspection tour, to
diseover an "opium den" among the Chinese members of the
crew. The inspeotors believed that a crew of about 30 should
generally be sufficient to man a vessel of her size. Com-
mmndod by the six British officers, including the captain,
however, they found a crew of 8l Chirese. In their search
of the vessel, the Inspector's attention was attracted by
strong-scented smoke issuing from one of the Chinese orews
quarters. within, they found some 10 Chinese lolling in
their bunks and amoking opium pipes. Pressing investiga-
tion, they disoovered that about half of the Chinese
crew were oplum addiots and were in the habit of using
this room seoretly.
with a customs official in aitendance, the water
police confiscated smoking articles and several pounds
of the drug. A protest to the captain (according to the
OSAKA MAINICHI) brought the reply that due to the faot
that half of the Chinese orew were opium addicts, their
offioleney was very low and that they became lax in their
work as soon as their stimulus ran low. It was therefore
neeessary to have twice the number of workers than usual.

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No
to despatch
No/203 of MAncH 19,/935 from tho
Embassy at Tokyo;
COPY
Translation.
No. 15/T.3.
Department of Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo, March 6, 1935.
Excellency:
With reference to the inquiries made by the American
Consul at Kobe to the Governor of Hyogo Prefecture in a
note (see copy herewith marked A) which was addressed to the
Governor under date of November 2, 1934, regarding the
smuggling of morphine into the Netherlands East Indies by
members of the crew of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha steamship
CHERIBON MARU, I have the honor to inform Your Excellency
that I have just received a report (see copy herewith
marked B) from the Governor of Hyogo-Ken through the Depart-
ment of Home Affairs. I beg Your Excellency will kindly
transmit the same to the American Consul at Kobe.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your
Excellency the assurances of my highest consideration.
Koki Hirota (Seal)
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
His Excellency
Mr. Joseph C. Grew,
Ambassador Extraordinary and Plenipotentiary
of the United States of America,
Tokyo.

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No
to despatch
No/203ot Mnch19,19Bomthe
Embassy at Tokyo.
COPY
AMERICAN CONSULATE SERVICE
AMERICAN CONSULATE, KOBE, JAPAN, NOVEMBER 2, 1934.
(A) Report.
His Excellency
T. Shirane,
Governor of Hyogo-Ken,
Kobe.
Excellency:
The Consulate has noted with interest an account in the
Japan Chronicle of October 25th to the effect that the Kobe
Water Police have arrested three members of the crew of the
N.Y.K. CHERIBON MARU on the charge of smuggling morphine
into the Netherland East Indies. The men arrested are stated
to be Chief Steward Michikado Iwamoto and Seamen Kinjiro Sasa-
moto and Masato Shigata.
Since the traffic in narcotic drugs is of an international
charecter, my Government maintains a dossier of all known drug
smugglers the world over. It would therefore be greatly appre-
ciated if your Government would furnish the Consulate with in-
formation along the following lines:
1.
The country of origin of the drugs smuggled.
2.
The places where the drugs were sold.
3.
The amount of narcotics thus disposed of.
4.
The period during which this activity went on.
5.
The previous criminal record, if any, of the
accused.
6.
The punishment which will be adjudged for the
smugglers if they were convicted.
With the assurance of my very great appreciation for
your cooperation and with evidence of my desire to reciprocate
your courtesy, I have the honor to be, Excellency,
Your obedient servant,
(signed)
Howard Donovan
American Consul.

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No
to despatch
No1203 of/March 19,1935from the
Embassy at Tokyo:
COPY
(B) Report.
February 2, 1935.
To the Director,
Sanitary Bureau,
Department of Home Affairs.
Inquiries concerning the smuggling
of Morphine from American Consul, Mr.
Howard Donovan, of the American Con-
sulate at Kobe.
Regarding the inquiries made by the American Consul
to the Hyogo prefectural office in a note (copy herewith)
under date of November 2, 1934, in which it is mentioned
that a quantity of morphine was smuggled into the Netherlands
East Indies by chief steward Michikado Iwamoto and other members
of the crew of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha steamship CHERIBON MARU,
all of whom were arrested by the Water Police of Hyogo prefec-
ture in October, 1934, the following are the results of in-
quiries made by the appropriate authorities:
(1) Country of origin of the drugs smuggled:
Japan (Kobe)
(2) Places where the drugs were sold: Surabaya and Batavia.
(3) Amount of narcotics this disposed of: 13 pounds.
(4) Period during which this activity went on: From
September 21, 1932, to April 9, 1933.
(5) Previous criminal record, if any, of the accused:
Michikado Iwamoto, former chief steward
of the CHERIBON MARU.
Kinjiro Sasamoto, former boatswain of the same
vessel
Yutaro Kikuchi, former member of the crew of
the same vessel
Shoto Shigeta, former watchman of the storerooms
of the same vessel.

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Kanichi Kimura, a tobacco dealer
(now deceased)
All of the above-mentioned men have no
previous criminal record.
(6) Prescribed penalty: All the accused are subject to
imprisonment with hard labor not to exceed
three months on the ground that they violated
the provisions of Article 10 of the narcotics
control regulations issued by the Department
of Home Affairs.
(7) Punishment: Iwamoto, Sasamoto and Kikuchi were
sentenced to one month's imprisonment with
hard labor each by the Kobe district court
on December 7, 1934, but they have appealed
against the verdict. Shigeta escaped and con-
sequently prosecution is temporarily delayed.
Kimura is dead and the right of prosecution
therefore has expired.
Michio Yuzawa,
Governor of Hyogo-Ken.
